Runtime: `actorFaceState` renders a lit 144×144 portrait (camera-side key light; repaints at 0/0.7/2.5s for texture decode; windows that drew too early are refreshed via a waiters list), `Window_StatusBase.drawActorFace` wrapper at the END of reactor_windows.js; `updateActorModelSprite` + `Sprite_Actor` update/updateBitmap/updateFrame wrappers at the END of reactor_sprites.js render the battler slot into `_mainSprite` (full-bitmap frame, stock motion cells bypassed, no SV sheet needed on disk; `playActorBattlerAnimation` queues actions). Triggers: `walking` and `dashing` join the rule/clip trigger set — `Reactor3D.moveTriggerActive` grades them, clip pick prefers the most specific gait then falls back to `moving`, spin gain and pose blends share the gate, the map sync passes `character.isDashing()` (followers mirror the party leader), and the sim bar gains a Dash toggle. Picker fixes: skinned models measured as an empty Box3 so animated GLBs were never centred (feet-at-origin left them towering off-frame) — centring now uses the template's measured size; `aimCamera` adds +0.5 to x/z (cell centres), so targets pass −0.5 to aim the true origin (picker and DB thumbnails both). Verified live end to end: three checkboxes and retitled bound boxes, face-framing sliders, Carol's lit 3D portrait in the pause menu, the engineer standing as the actor's battler in battle under the full plugin stack, and Fleagus dead-centre in the picker. New phrases ship curated in the 17 locales.

- **Real-time animation clock, rig-mode orbit, clip speed.** The Database 3D preview's frame counter incremented per rAF tick — a 120Hz display doubled every rule and clip; it now derives frames from `performance.now()` and `applyModelAnimation` runs once per animation frame (spin and walk-distance gains accumulate per call). The runtime's clip mixer steps by the caller's frame delta instead of a fixed 1/60 per call (`binding.clipFrame`), so any call rate plays clips at true speed. Rig mode adds `'rig'` to the orbit predicate — presses that miss every marker orbit; marker hits still drag. Clip rules carry a `rate` field (0.25–3, default 1, omitted when 1): `clipAction.timeScale`, duration scaled in `modelRuleDuration`, a **Speed** percent slider on the card, wired through defaultWork/snapshot/editRule/savePose. And `renderEditCard`'s parts gate now counts embedded clips — a clip-only GLB (no parts, no rig) hid the card entirely, which is why adopted clips seemed to have no settings. Verified live: rig-drag orbits 38°, clock 59.9fps, saved rate 2 halves the rule duration (62→31 frames), same-frame mixer step is exactly 0.

- **Embedded clips surface in the Animations panel, and skinned GLB clips play undistorted.** `renderRuleList` appends an **Embedded clips** section for a clip-bearing GLB: per clip, ▶ plays it through the sim's `__preview` action with a transient clip rule (no authoring needed), and ＋ adopts it as a saved `{name, type:'clip', clip, trigger:'action'}` rule — named after the clip so `playModelAnimation`/`playBattlerAnimation` and the sim bar pick it up; an adopted clip shows ✓ and its row disables. Underneath, `buildAnimatedGlbTemplate` now binds skinned meshes with an **identity bindMatrix**: three applies `boneWorld · IBM · bindMatrix` per vertex and glTF's inverse binds already map mesh space to joint space, so binding with the mesh node's world mixed the armature transform in twice — harmless-looking at rest (a uniform shrink the camera framing absorbed: the reduced Captain Everson measured 1.7cm), catastrophic in motion (bones correct, skinning shredded — 72/72 tracks bound and sane while the render exploded). Verified live on her six Meshy clips (raw mixer and rule-engine paths both clean) and pinned by a synthetic cm-armature skinning test that fails 66× out of range under the old bind.

- **Database 3D bindings: actors, enemies, weapons, armor, and items take a model.** A shared `Database3DBindings` helper owns `data/Database.r3d.json` ({ actors/enemies/weapons/armors/items: { id: spec } }, spec identical to a map sidecar's event entry; clearing the last binding deletes the file) and renders the 3D Model row — checkbox, bound-model name, Change Model — on all five detail pages through `ModelGraphicPicker` (handed a shim carrying mapEditor3D so the preview can load three.js — the database editors' bare project shim left it black); the picker's cancel path never calls back, so the row watches for the modal leaving the DOM and resyncs from the sidecar. The runtime loads the sidecar once (disk-stat first, so its absence never logs), `characterModelSpec` answers for `Game_Player` (party leader's actor binding) and `Game_Follower` (their own actor's), the model sync includes the player and visible followers (instance keys `p` and `f<index>`), and `normalizeModelSpec` is the single raw-entry-to-validated-spec path shared with event models. In battle, `Sprite_Enemy.update` hands bound enemies to `updateEnemyModelSprite`: an offscreen shared WebGL renderer draws the posed, lit model into the sprite's bitmap every frame, ambient rules play on the battler's own clock, `playBattlerAnimation(enemyId, name)` queues named actions, and an enemy transform rebuilds the state — the wrapper is installed after the class body, since `Sprite_Enemy`'s prototype is replaced wholesale at definition and an early wrapper dies with it. Rig markers carry canvas-sprite name labels (center yellow, left blue, right red) that ride marker drags with their mirrored twins; hover raycasts skip entirely during orbit/marker drags and on a stationary pointer, and the raycast interval scales with the model's triangle count — a 3.1M-triangle Meshy character orbits at ~6 ms a frame where it previously hitched. Verified end to end over CDP: the row on all five pages, picker open/cancel/resync, binding round-trip showing the model name, the rigged engineer walking as the player with 17 bones and Walk rules on the map, both goblins of troop 1 rendering as ready 3D battlers at 60 fps under the Demo's full 44-plugin battle stack, and marker labels on screen. A bound enemy needs no battler art on disk: the stock image load is skipped and a placeholder bitmap stands in until the model render takes over (plugins read the bitmap for state-icon placement). New phrases (35 marker labels and Change Model) ship curated in the 17 non-English locales.

- **Static humanoid models rig inside the editor — no external tools.** Database > 3D grows a **Rig** tool: thirteen draggable joint markers (mirrored left/right, color-coded, with the derived 17-bone skeleton drawn live between them) fit the standard humanoid to any static model, and **Bind rig** solves skin weights right there — distance-to-bone falloff with a side gate that keeps a left thigh off the right leg, seam welding so UV-split duplicate vertices can never crack open when posed, and Laplacian smoothing over the mesh's own edges. The solve is fast enough to feel instant (218 ms for the 30,940-vertex Demo engineer) and the model reappears skinned on the spot. Every bone then **is a part on the pose card**: click the model and the dominant bone under the cursor is picked from its skin weights, the card's Pose/Swing/Spin/Bob sliders bend it live about its own joint, parent bones carry children exactly as a skeleton should (the same ancestry composition the tank's turret uses — a bone's local rotation simply *is* forward kinematics), and held poses, timed effects, previews, and undo all work unchanged because bones ride the existing rule engine rather than a new one. The rig persists in the model's own `model.json` — skeleton plus quantized weights, about 330 KB for the engineer — and the game performs the identical bind at load: verified end-to-end in a live run where the editor bound the engineer, saved a Raise-Arm animation, and the running Demo loaded the model skinned, played the animation through `Play Model Animation`'s pipeline (a shoulder vertex measurably swinging 11.5 model units), and eased back to exactly rest. A model carries a rig **or** carved parts, never both — both count triangles over the uncarved meshes — and the editor says so instead of letting them scramble each other. All new UI text ships in the 17 non-English locales.

- **MP3, WAV, FLAC, and M4A are first-class audio formats alongside OGG.** Project data stays extensionless exactly as MZ stores it; the actual format resolves at load time. In the runtime, `WebAudio._startLoading` resolves the on-disk extension up front through `Utils.resolveAudioExtension` (NW.js, alongside the existing case resolution — so plugins constructing `WebAudio` directly inherit it), while web deploys walk the other extensions in `_onError` only when a request died with zero bytes received, then restore the original URL for honest error reporting. `_readLoopComments` dispatches on container magic: OGG unchanged, WAV reads the `fmt ` sample rate and first `smpl` sampler loop, MP3 reads ID3v2.3/2.4 `TXXX` `LOOPSTART`/`LOOPLENGTH` frames with the sample rate from the first MPEG frame header, FLAC reads STREAMINFO and its vorbis comments — all bounds-checked because streamed loads parse a growing prefix, and a truncated-prefix `decodeAudioData` failure no longer counts as a load error (generation-guarded; WAV prefixes genuinely fail where OGG's tolerate). The Vorbis fallback decoder is now gated to `.ogg` URLs. In the editor, every audio surface (Audio Player, audio commands, System sounds, Animation SE, movement-route SE) lists all formats through a shared `RRAssetFiles.AUDIO_EXTENSIONS`, ranked so OGG shadows a same-named twin (MV's `.ogg`+`.m4a` pairs keep listing once); `AudioCoverArt` additionally extracts MP3 `APIC` frames (front cover preferred) and native FLAC `PICTURE` blocks; encrypted variants (`.mp3_`, `.wav_`, `.flac_`) decrypt everywhere the OGG ones did. `audioFileExt()` still answers `.ogg` for plugins that ask. Verified in a live NW.js boot: all four formats resolve from a plain `.ogg` request, decode, and report correct loop points and sample rates.

- **Every audio picker is the Audio Player's interface, served by one shared modal.** New `RRAudioPickerModal` (src/utils/AudioPickerModal.js) carries the player's own classes: a cover-art header (`track-art-large`) naming the selected track, the fine-step seek row, play/pause/stop/loop `audio-player-button`s beside optional `audio-inline-control` volume/pitch/pan cards, and the alphabet rail with letter-section headers and 26px cover thumbnails per row — lazy-loaded through the same IntersectionObserver pattern and the Audio Player's shared cover cache and locale-aware track ordering (`window.reactor.audioPlayer`, with local fallbacks). Clicking a row selects and previews it, double-click confirms. Four surfaces route through it: the System 1 slots (title/battle/vehicle BGM, ME slots, system SE list — level cards shown, replacing the per-row play buttons and bare slider grid), the audio event commands' Browse picker (cards hidden since the command dialog owns those fields; previews at the command's current levels, and picking (None) now clears the command), the movement route's Play SE dialog (fully replaced — its dropdown-plus-sliders dialog is gone), and Map Properties' autoplay BGM/BGS via a new "…" button beside each dropdown that writes name and levels back into the form. The single `releaseAudio` path closes the preview AudioContext on every exit, keeping all four surfaces under Chromium's per-page context cap. Shared styling: the `.audio-player-info` card is panel-black behind a `--color-accent` border, and `.audio-scroll` lists get an accent-gradient scrollbar pill on a `--color-bg-deep` channel — the Audio Player itself inherits both. The Demo's 65 Psychronic BGM tracks carry embedded `METADATA_BLOCK_PICTURE` covers (art sourced from the MP3 masters; oversized art recompressed to 512px so extraction stays within the prefix-read ceiling).

- **The Audio Player's BGM/BGS/ME/SE tabs read as tabs.** Dormant tabs were the modal background with no outline; they now sit as bordered near-black chips (`--color-bg-panel` + `--color-border`), hover in the accent tint, and keep the accent highlight when active — all from theme variables, so every palette in both modes gets its own version.

- **The Audio Player shows album art and packs its controls into one row.** New `AudioCoverArt` util parses the OGG Vorbis/Opus comment header for embedded pictures (`METADATA_BLOCK_PICTURE` FLAC blocks and the legacy `COVERART`/`COVERARTMIME` pair), reading only a growing prefix of the file — `RREncryptedAssets.readAssetBytes` serves those prefixes for plain, encrypted, and case-mismatched files, since only the first 16 bytes are XORed. The header shows a 56px cover beside the track name and every track row a 26px thumbnail, loaded lazily via IntersectionObserver as rows scroll into view and cached per path; tracks without art get a deterministic name-hued placeholder tile. Volume/pitch/pan moved out of their grid card into the playback-button row as inline slider groups, and the seek sliders (Audio Player and both AudioCommandEditor previews) step at 0.1% instead of the default 1% snap.

### Changed

- **PixiJS updated to 8.20.0** (from 8.14.0), in the editor's dependencies and the bundled runtime (`runtime/libs/pixi.js`, synced to every template project). The internals the compat layer patches (`_gpuData`, `_activeBlendMode`, the back-buffer filter path, TexturePool) are unchanged in 8.20. Verified live: the Demo boots to title and renders a 3D map cleanly, and Star Shift Rebellion's plugin-heavy map title renders through the MV compat layer — both with zero console errors — and the full editor test suite passes.

### Fixed

- **Event clipboard and undo carry the 3D model sidecar.** `EventManager` copy/cut attach `map.reactor3d.events[id]` to the clipboard payload (`ReactorClipboard` included, so cross-window paste works), paste writes the entry under the new event's id, delete purges the entry (`getNextEventId` reuses freed ids — a stale entry would attach its model to a future unrelated event), and `saveState`/`undo`/`redo` snapshot and restore `{ events, models }` together (old bare-array stack entries still restore). Covered by a behavioral test driving the real class through copy → paste → delete → undo → redo → cut → paste.

- **Light themes pass a real contrast audit.** A WCAG sweep of every text-on-background pairing across all seven palettes found the light modes leaning on accent tones picked for dark backgrounds — the default light theme's gold `--color-accent-hover` sat at 2:1 as text on white (track names, letter headers, link-colored labels were the "hard to read" spots), and several palettes put white button text on accents that only reach ~3:1 (creamsicle's orange was worst at 2.85). Light-block accents are darkened until accent-as-text and white-on-accent both clear 4.5:1 (`--color-accent`, `--color-accent-hover`, and dim placeholder text, per palette), with dark themes untouched. The toolbar's pixel-art icons — bright cyan drawn for dark button faces — get a light-theme-scoped `filter` pulling them toward their outline color so they read as ink instead of neon on cream. The About dialog also now credits Three.js alongside NW.js and PixiJS v8, in all 18 locales.

- **The map grid no longer loses lines as the view zooms out.** The grid is drawn in map space inside the zoomed container, so its width-1 stroke shrank with the view — at 50% zoom every line was half a device pixel wide and rasterized away (measured live: 0 of 50 expected lines at 50% zoom, 2 at 25%). The stroke now uses PIXI 8's `pixelLine`, which stays exactly one device pixel through any transform: the full line count renders at every zoom level, and the grid also stops fattening past 100%.

- **The tileset palette empties stale slots when the map's tileset changes.** `TilesetPaletteViewer.loadTilesetImages` skipped empty `tilesetNames` entries without clearing the per-slot texture cache, so creating or opening a map whose tileset leaves a slot blank (no A3, no C, ...) kept rendering the previous tileset's sheet in that slot. `loadTilesetForMap` now resets the cache and cached layer canvas on every load, clears the selection only when the tileset actually changed (map switches sharing a tileset keep the pick), and carries a load token so a slow superseded load can neither commit its sheets nor repaint the palette after a newer switch.

- **Asset filename casing resolves before the request instead of in the error handler.** The 0.98.2 recovery lived in `Bitmap._onError`/`WebAudio._onError`, which plugins may replace without calling the original — CGMZ_Fallback does exactly that, so on case-sensitive filesystems a Windows-authored case mismatch (`npc_female2` vs `NPC_female2.png_`) became the fallback placeholder forever plus perpetual `ERR_FILE_NOT_FOUND` console spam. `Utils.resolveFileCase` now corrects the URL at every request point (plain bitmap, encrypted bitmap, audio) with a single `existsSync` gate keeping the exact-match fast path to one stat; the `_onError` retry remains as a backstop. Verified live in Haven: the three mismatched NPC sheets load their real 624×576 art with zero console errors.

- **A `<3d>` map with no painted sidecar no longer logs a failed request on every entry.** `DataManager.loadMapSidecar` asked the network for `Map###.r3d.json` and treated the 404 as the normal "render flat" answer — correct behavior, but the browser logs `ERR_FILE_NOT_FOUND` at a level the page cannot suppress, so testing Star Shift Rebellion's Map596 spammed the console on each visit. Under NW.js the loader now stats the sidecar on disk first and skips the request entirely when the file is absent; a sidecar that exists is requested and parsed exactly as before (verified live against SSR's Map596/Map612 pair).

- **Class parameter curves author and display the engine's real 999-level domain.** Reactor's level cap is 999, but the Classes graphs plotted only the stored MZ levels 1–99 and the Generate Curve modal stopped at a "Level 99 value" slider. Both graphs now plot the exact series the game computes at every level 1–999 — stored values where the array has them, the runtime's linear extrapolation at the final slope beyond, drawn dashed and dimmer past a faint divider so projected growth reads as projected. The modal's second slider authors the **Level 999 value** directly (label curated in all 17 locales), the readout reports Lv1/Lv99/Lv500/Lv999, and Apply writes every level per-level, so what the curve shows at 999 is authored rather than an accident of the slope at 99; HP/MP bounds rise to 99,999 and other stats to 9,999 to fit the taller domain. Legacy 100-entry arrays still open (the cap value seeds from the extrapolation, the shape infers from whatever domain is stored) and still play identically — the runtime reads exact stored values first and extrapolates only past the array, so no runtime change was needed. Verified live over CDP: the mini graphs ink the full width, and Apply commits a 1000-entry array whose Lv999 matches the modal.

- **The Classes dialogs wear the editor's standard modal chrome.** The Learnable Skill editor was a bare padded box with an inline heading, the Trait editor rolled its own header and footer with a hard-coded dark tab bar (`#252525`, wrong in light themes), and their footers mixed three button styles between them. All four dialogs on the Classes screen — Learnable Skill, Trait, Generate Curve, and EXP Curve — now use `rr-modal-header`/`rr-modal-body`/`rr-modal-footer` with a secondary Cancel and a primary OK/Apply, the tab bars sit on `--color-bg-panel`, and the Trait editor's title says Add or Edit to match the action. Trait rows across all six tabs share one grid (radio | label | control | prefix | value | unit), so every select, number, and % sign lands on the same vertical lines — including through the dropdown shim wrapper, which had collapsed selects to their shortest option. Verified live: all rows compute the same six grid columns on every tab.

- **Trait sections' Add/Edit/Copy/Paste/Delete are the shared chip buttons.** Classes, Actors, Weapons, Armors, States, and Enemies all styled those five buttons inline with JS hover handlers, visibly mismatching the Learnable Skills, Effects, and Action Patterns strips beside them. All six editors now use `rr-btn-chip` with CSS-driven hover and disabled states, and the hand-rolled hover/enable juggling is gone.

- **An app-wide modal and button consistency pass.** Every OK/Save/Apply confirm button that was styled inline with accent colors — 65 event-command dialogs, the plugin manager, the audio picker, the animation picker, the battle-test dialog, System pages, the movement-route editor, and the Troop and Common Event command dialogs' shared button factory — is now the one `rr-button-primary` class, with the leftover JS hover handlers (which fought the class's own hover) removed. The Skill/Item **Effect editor** was rebuilt on the same modal chrome and shared grid rows as the Trait editor (its hard-coded `#252525` tab bar is a theme token now), and the **enemy Action Pattern editor** gained the standard header and footer bars in place of a bare padded box.

- **Every event command dialog and the remaining database dialogs wear the standard modal chrome.** All ~85 event-command editors carried hand-rolled copies of the header, title, close button, and footer as inline styles; they now use the `rr-modal` chrome classes (84 titles, 81 close buttons, and 141 header/footer bars converted by pattern, plus the Event Command and Animation pickers and the map Find Event dialog). On the Troops page, the enemy picker, the page Conditions dialog, the raw-parameter fallback dialog (Common Events' twin included), and the Battle Test configuration all moved onto the same chrome with close buttons where they were missing, the sidebar's Battle Test and Members/page-toolbar buttons became the shared secondary and chip classes, and the Animation editor's SE picker, Effekseer effect picker, and cell context menu dropped their one-off chrome — including a hard-coded `#3a3a3a` hover that ignored light themes. The Deploy Game and Deploy Editor wizards joined too: standard header bars, and their Start/Cancel Build buttons moved out of the log column into a proper footer bar with the primary/secondary styles.

- **Deploy Game compresses every audio format, opt-in per deploy.** The "Re-encode OGG audio" option is now **Compress audio (lossy)**: one checkbox and one quality choice covering everything the engine plays — OGG re-encodes through libvorbis at the chosen quality as before, MP3 through LAME VBR and M4A through AAC at bitrates mapped from the same scale, and WAV and FLAC convert to OGG Vorbis, the format the runtime already prefers when several carry one name. Loop points survive every path: OGG/FLAC vorbis comments and MP3 `TXXX` tags carry through the re-encode and are verified afterwards, and a WAV's `smpl` sampler loop is written into the produced OGG as `LOOPSTART`/`LOOPLENGTH` using exactly the runtime's semantics — any file whose loop tags fail verification, or whose result isn't smaller, ships untouched, and a WAV/FLAC shadowed by a same-named OGG is left alone. The encoder checks confirm libvorbis, libmp3lame, and aac in the verified FFmpeg build only for the formats the project actually contains. Because the compression is lossy, the checkbox now starts unchecked on every deploy instead of restoring a saved preference (the quality choice is still remembered), and the settings note spells out the per-format behavior in all 17 locales. The deploy wizards' scroll areas also join the accent-pill scrollbar treatment with a proper gutter, so options no longer sit flush against a near-invisible scrollbar.

- **Modals and database pages behave at small window sizes.** `rr-modal` is globally capped at the viewport (`max-width: calc(100vw - 16px)`), 65 event-command dialogs cap their fixed pixel widths with `min(...)` plus a `max-height` and a scrolling body, and the Actors page's section pairs and the Classes page's columns moved from inline grid styles into CSS — so the existing `database-detail` container query can collapse them to one column when the pane narrows, verified live (2 columns wide, 1 at 700px). The trait/effect modal grid also tightens its columns below 540px.

- **No more awkward voids in paired database cards.** On the Actors page, General Settings ended mid-card while the Images card ran long, and the Traits card floated its buttons above dead space. The card-fill chain (section → content → form) now covers the Actors pairs: the Profile textarea absorbs the leftover height exactly as Description does on Skills — a grow-row alignment fix (`align-items: end` from the shared row rule was pinning the field to the bottom of its grown row) applies to every filled card — and list-card action strips (traits, learnable skills, effects, action patterns) pin to the bottom edge of a stretched card so a short list reads as free list area. In the Trait editor, rows without a dropdown (Attack Speed, Attack Times+, Action Times+) place their number where the dropdown column sits instead of leaving the middle of the row empty.

- **Legacy pixi-filters (v5 era) run on the PIXI 8 runtime.** Plugins bundling the old filter pack (Kawase blur, Godray, DropShadow, MultiColorReplace, SimpleLightmap, ...) override `Filter.apply` with multi-pass bodies that call v5 FilterSystem APIs, which black-screened Haven/Project3 the first time a scene enabled one. The mv_compat bridge now covers the whole surface: `_frame`/`filterFrame` aliases on textures (assignable, matching v5), `getFilterTexture`/`returnFilterTexture` mapped onto the v8 TexturePool and sized from the running filter pass, array uniforms with literal or `const int`/`#define` sizes carried into the uniform group, extra `sampler2D` uniforms routed to shader resources through non-enumerable accessors (a texture key the uniform-sync generator can see but not type crashes it), a rename for shaders that use `finalColor` as their own local before PIXI 8 claimed that name, and v5 `(callback, scope)` ObservablePoint construction. Verified in the live NW.js runtime: all 41 filter classes in Project3's bundled plugin construct and render with no errors.
- **Web builds no longer crash on a 3D-bound actor's retired sprite sheet.** `Database.r3d.json` reads synchronously on NW.js but is an async fetch in a browser, so the first web frames didn't yet know an actor was 3D and requested the 2D walking sheet the project no longer ships — "Failed to load img/characters/...". `Scene_Boot` now waits for the sidecar answer (the fetch always settles with success or failure, so boot cannot hang), and a player or follower sprite never commits to a sheet while the answer is pending — it retries the next frame instead. Verified by booting the Demo with the sidecar artificially delayed two seconds: boot held, the stale sheet was never requested, and both bound actors reached the map as models.
- **Deploy audio compression keeps embedded cover art.** FFmpeg's demuxers surface embedded art (an Ogg `METADATA_BLOCK_PICTURE` comment, an ID3 `APIC` frame, a FLAC `PICTURE` block) as an attached picture stream and delete the tag it came from, so every audio re-encode shipped artless music while faithfully preserving loop tags. The optimizer now extracts the picture before encoding and writes it back — rebuilt as a `METADATA_BLOCK_PICTURE` comment for Ogg outputs (through an ffmetadata file, never argv, which Windows caps at 32K characters), or as a copied attached-picture stream for MP3 and M4A. A failed with-art encode retries without art rather than failing the deploy. The Demo soundtrack's art, dropped by the 0.98.3 size pass, was restored losslessly from the pre-compression files (66 of 68 tracks; the other two never had art).

- **The MV-compat stale-event guard no longer eats plugin-spawned events.** The guard drops save-restored events whose map data no longer exists — but it judged staleness by looking the event's id up in `$dataMap` directly, and action/spawner plugins (Hendrix Action Engine item drops, template events, DotMoveSystem companions) legitimately add runtime events at ids beyond the authored map. Every `Game_Map.refresh` deleted them and the console spammed "dropped N stale event(s)" while drops vanished off the ground. The guard now asks the event itself (`event()`, which dynamic-event classes override to serve their own data): an event whose own data comes back empty or throws is stale; one serving plugin data is alive. Verified in the Hendrix Action Combat project: a full boot–play–transfer run that previously warned continuously now runs silent with the spawned events intact.
- **Tilemap teardown no longer warns about a destroyed texture still bound to a shader.** PIXI 8's mesh pipe keeps the last-drawn texture in its shared shader after the final draw, so destroying the tile atlas on map transfer logged "[BindGroup] a 'textureSource' was destroyed while still bound to a shader" every time. The atlas release now points those shader slots at the empty texture before destroying the source — guarded so a renderer without that private shape simply keeps the harmless warning.

- **Render-guard warnings now say which resource died.** The SpritePipe guard that skips destroyed/orphan sprites (instead of letting PIXI 8 crash the render pass to a black screen) warned with the sprite's class and parent chain but not the cause. It now names the exact reason — destroyed, `_gpuData` nulled by a destroy, or a destroyed texture source — and every Bitmap's GPU texture source is labeled with its file path (or canvas size), so "texture source destroyed [img/system/Window.png]" points straight at the leaked resource. Verified live in the Haven test project: labels resolve to real asset paths on every scene sprite.
- **Album art shows in the web editor too.** The cover-art reader sniffs a small prefix of the audio file through synchronous byte access, which a browser does not have — on itch.io it silently returned nothing and every track wore its placeholder tile. In web mode the same prefix walk now runs over `fetch` with a Range header, streaming and cancelling at the read cap when the host ignores ranges, so a 13KB cover never downloads a 5MB track. Both call sites already resolve() the reader's return value, so the promise the web path returns is adopted without any UI changes; pinned by a test that serves a real Demo track through a Range-ignoring fake server and asserts the extraction and the byte cap.

## [0.98.2] - 2026-08-16

### Added

- **3D models animate through authored rules, and the Database has a 3D section to author them.** A model folder carries `model.json` naming its animation rules — spin (wheels roll with distance travelled and hold their angle at rest), swing (a hinge about a named part's own pivot), and bob — triggered always, at idle, while moving, or on demand by name. The readers preserve named-part ancestry and pivots (GLB before its hierarchy is baked flat; OBJ by splitting named groups into their own meshes), so one rule like `DEF-Wheel` turns all four wheels of a car about their axles. Database → 3D lists every model folder in the project, discovers its part names from the mesh — scene-graph plumbing (root/rig/scene wrappers that grab every mesh at once) and exporters' anonymous "Object" bucket are kept out of the list, and numbered siblings collapse into one group option (DEF-Wheel drives all four wheels) — edits the rule list with a live animated preview (Walk simulation, a Play button per action), and writes each model's own sidecar on every change, no hand-written JSON. Selecting a rule draws a box around exactly the meshes it drives with a live affected-part count, edits restart accumulated spin from rest, and a GLB's embedded clip count is surfaced. **Embedded clips play.** A GLB that carries animations keeps its node hierarchy live instead of being baked flat: joints stay real objects, skinned meshes ride a GPU skeleton, every instance rebinds to its own cloned bones, and each embedded clip is parsed into a playable animation. A "Clip" rule type in the 3D section plays a chosen clip through the same trigger system — always, at idle, while moving, or on demand from the Play Model Animation command — with crossfades between states; one-shot actions return to the ambient clip when done. A model whose clips are static poses (common in ripped character models — sas blue's 394 are an eye-test pose library) gets a pose system from the same rules. Sizing an animated model measures a CPU-skinned sample of its rest pose, the only size matching what renders — bone positions undersized a Source-style rig to a speck. The model list in the 3D section now uses the standard database list styling with a search box. A new Play Model Animation event command (under a fourth Reactor tab in the command picker, where Reactor-only commands will grow) triggers an action on this event, the player, or any event; it is stored as an MZ plugin command for "RPGReactor", so the data stays loadable everywhere and a stock MZ runtime simply ignores it. In the Demo, the Buick's wheels roll as it drives, and the monster-plant sways, pecks at idle, and bites on command.

- **Event Image can author a 3D model into the map sidecar.** Checking 3D opens `ModelGraphicPicker`: folder names under `3d/<folder>/source`, a WebGL orbit preview with the same rotation gizmo as the Animation Generator, size in tiles, and four placeable facing dots rather than snap-yaw buttons. Apply writes `map.reactor3d.events[id][page]`; the Event editor preview fills the leftover left column and turns with the page's Down/Left/Right/Up. Runtime `characterModelSpec` prefers that sidecar over `<r3d>` notes. Models occupy their ground AABB, do not block themselves, refuse a turn that would hit the player, and draw other characters as upright billboards so they pass in front or behind the mesh. A model collides as its true rotated rectangle at the yaw the mesh actually stands at — the authored spec rotation plus the front-mark aim, not the facing alone — so a character walks right up to an angled vehicle's body instead of being stopped tiles away at a bounding-box corner, and a model posed with an authored yaw no longer collides crosswise to its visible body. A gliding model keeps its trailing tiles occupied, a step that implies a turn is collision-tested in both body orientations against the player and other solid events, the mesh eases through turns at `MODEL_TURN_SPEED` instead of pivoting instantly — and a turn must clear the disc the body's corners sweep, in both swing directions, with the footprint covering the arc while the easing plays out — and character billboards draw right side up (three's default `flipY`, not the glTF convention). Leaned billboards — character sprites and tile cut-outs alike — write the depth of a vertical twin at their anchor (`straightenBillboardDepth`), so front/behind against real meshes settles per pixel by ground row instead of the lean tipping a sprite's head into the model behind it. On such maps the star-flagged tiles render inside that same depth buffer rather than as a flat composite pass, so a character in front of a tall structure occludes its top and one behind it is hidden; a stationary event on a facade cell — any priority — snaps to that wall's plane and is pulled just ahead of the coplanar wall art, so a console screen shows whole over its machine while a genuinely nearer character still occludes it by real depth, in front winning and behind hiding exactly as the camera sees it. A walking character standing on a facade's own footprint — pressed against a console, crossing a machine's apron rows — has their depth pushed just in front of that wall's plane, since the art on their cell draws under them in 2D; off the footprint, genuinely behind still means hidden. Character billboards also take the tile cut-outs' half-cell step towards the camera, and an above-characters event whose cell was stood into a facade anchors on that wall's recorded plane (`facadeAt`), lifted along the same leaning axis its quads use — so an event's art keeps the alignment it was authored with over tile art from every camera position instead of drifting off it towards the screen edges.

- **Looking around the model picker no longer edits the pose, and posing happens through rings around the model.** A plain drag used to rotate the model itself about camera-relative axes, so inspecting a model quietly baked pitch and roll into its authored angles and it stood crooked in game — which read as the face marks depending on the viewing angle; worse, once any roll existed a straight drag bled into all three angles at once and the model fought the hand. Left-drag orbits now, and the pose controls are three colour-coded rings wrapped around the model: grab a ring and the model follows the pointer around that one axis, the others untouched. The rings nest like a gimbal so each always matches the axis it will change, arcs passing behind the model hide behind it (a faint twin keeps the circle traceable), and they whisper until touched: dim at rest, brighter under the pointer, full only while held — when the other two fall away so the turn reads clearly. Placing a Front/Back/Left/Right dot hides the rings entirely, since the dots share the same colour palette — and an armed dot lands on a stationary left click again (making plain drags orbit had silently killed placement, since the old gate required the drag state that no longer exists). A near miss lands too: sparse meshes — foliage, struts — let the ray slip between triangles at spots the eye reads as on the model, and swallowing that click kept the mode armed with the rings away, so placement looked refused; the ray's entry into the model's bounds stands in, and a click past the bounds entirely — out where the rings circle — still lands on the model's side in the clicked direction. An armed canvas click is never swallowed — the dot lands the moment the button goes down, on the pressed pixel, so hand wobble can no longer turn a placement into an orbit. A drag that slips past the modal's edge also no longer cancels the picker outright (a browser click targets the common ancestor of press and release, so escaping the preview "clicked" the backdrop and threw away the dots); closing by backdrop needs the press and the release both out there. The floating gizmo ball and controls strip no longer stand between the pointer and the scene: the strip's transparent gaps pass clicks through, and while a side is armed the gizmo ball goes inert and dim so a dot click lands on the model behind it — in a small window those widgets sit right over the model's base, and they were catching the very clicks that looked like placement refusing to work. A grab also lands on the ring the eye sees: rings are picked by screen distance to the drawn line, tie-broken at crossings by whichever ring is drawn on top, where fat invisible grab tubes previously sent a third of direct clicks to a ring visibly away from the pointer. Ctrl-/right-drag keeps a turntable nudge (horizontal turns, vertical tips — never a camera-axis tumble), the X/Y/Z fields still take typed angles, and new Upright / Reset buttons rescue a drifted pose — Upright keeps the turn and stands the model up.
- **OBJ models render with their textures.** The OBJ reader now keeps texture coordinates (welding position/uv corners into one attribute), the picker names a colour map from the model's `textures/` folder — preferring the colour pass over normal/emissive companions — and the sidecar carries that choice to the runtime, so a plant that shipped as OBJ + JPG stands textured in the picker, the event editor's preview (which also derives the map for specs saved before the sidecar carried one), and the game. STL/FBX/DXF/3MF/USDZ gain the same texture path where their readers produce UVs.
- **Model files keep whatever extension case they shipped with.** Exporters write `Plant_001.OBJ`; the picker's lowercase-only listing rule hid such files and the folder read as empty, so OBJ models seemed unsupported. The model picker now lists any-case extensions and the sidecar records the real filename; a note-based spec with no extension also probes upper-case variants at load.
- **A 3D object spanning both palette halves selects and extends as one.** A B–G sheet is sixteen columns shown as two stacked eight-column halves, so a prop crossing column eight — a full-sheet reactor — is authored as two drags. Ctrl and Cmd now arm the extend gesture alongside Shift, the merged object's selection is stored in sheet coordinates and drawn as both display pieces, and after an extend the whole object stays highlighted instead of only the piece just added.
- **Arrow keys step through asset picker file lists.** In the character graphic picker — and every picker built on the shared file browser — Up/Down move the selection one file at a time with the preview following, scrolling the list only when the selection reaches its edge, instead of scrolling the pane out from under the reader. Home/End jump to the ends, and the keyboard is armed the moment the picker opens.
- **The tileset 3D preview turns when dragged and fits its pane.** Standing art is drawn as an upright plane that shears with the turning ground line, so a drag visibly swings the object instead of only sliding a parallelogram beneath a fixed picture; near edge-on it holds a readable width, as the in-game billboard does. The scale now answers to the pane's width, the ground's depth, and the slab's height at once — a fixed ten-pixel floor previously let a sixteen-row object tower off the top of the pane and read as off-centre.
- **The flag Key stays on screen while a tall sheet scrolls.** The Key column — and the passability brushes and 3D tool palette it hosts — is sticky within the sheet's scroll pane, so painting the bottom rows of a 32-row F sheet no longer means scrolling back up to switch marks.
- **The tileset passability Key doubles as a paint palette.** Clicking O, X, or ★ in the Key arms that mark: a click on the sheet writes it directly and a drag paints every tile the pointer crosses, with autotiles still mirroring across their 48 shapes. Clicking the armed mark again returns to the classic click-to-cycle. Re-crossing a tile mid-drag is idempotent, so a wobbly sweep cannot flip anything back.

### Fixed

- **Tiles no longer show crawling horizontal seams when the camera pans zoomed or at fractional positions.** The PIXI 8 tile mesh inset every tile's texture rect by half a texel, compressing 48 texel rows into a 47-row span — invisible at integer alignment, but the moment a camera plugin panned the map at fractional screen positions or any zoom was in play, one texel row per tile duplicated or dropped, and its position crawled with the pan ("warbly" seams). The inset is now a whisker (1/128 texel), just enough to keep a quad edge from sampling the neighbouring atlas row: at 2× zoom every texel row now doubles exactly, and integer-aligned rendering is pixel-identical to before.

- **A menu-background snapshot survives the next snap for plugin windows still showing it, and setting `blur` on a filter is quiet again.** MZ destroys the previous screen snapshot the moment a new one is taken; menu-background plugins cache it into their own window sprites (a game's save screen hit this), and under PIXI 8 the destroyed texture drops that sprite from the render with a destroyed/orphan console warning. Retired snapshots now pass through a three-deep trail before dying, so a cached background keeps drawing and memory stays bounded. PIXI 8 also kept `BlurFilter.blur` only as a deprecation wrapper that warns on use — VisuStella's picture effects set it every frame — so the compat layer replaces the accessor with a silent passthrough to `strength`.

- **The Web editor bundle fits browser storefronts' 1000-file upload cap again.** The zip had grown past it, and the bulk was library stock the bundled project never touches: battle-hud face skins for actors the project does not define, source .psd files, and a hundred unreferenced stock enemy battlers. The web packager now trims exactly those — the audio library, fog pack, and everything the sandbox browses stay — dropping the archive from over a thousand files to roughly 700. Extracted size comes down the same way: 3D model folders no map places (a 77MB rip among them) leave the web bundle, and the Character Generator's bulk-imported looseleaf style pack — 76MB of generated part data — stays out of the web editor bundle, which shrinks from 87MB to 11MB and parses at boot accordingly; the compact psychronic style ships, and the soundtrack is untouched. The extracted bundle lands near 426MB. Counts and sizes are a storefront's rule, not the build's: packaging always completes, reports its file count and extracted size, and warns past 1000 files or 500MB. Shipped templates also exclude any live project lock or playtest saves. The web editor also loads again at all: the desktop runtime-refresh check probed project files with synchronous reads the browser host reserves for preloaded files, and the uncaught throw froze the web editor on its splash screen — the web bundle bakes its runtime in at packaging time, so the check now stands down in the browser. And because browser-saved edits shadow the bundled project, a returning player kept seeing the OLD Reactor One after an update — its new content missing or broken; the bundle is stamped now, and when the stamp changes under stored edits the editor offers to load the new version up front instead of silently serving stale files.

- **A native-Windows GPU crash on the 3D map toggle now diagnoses and heals itself.** Checking 3D could take the whole editor down instantly on Windows proper (while fine under Wine/Proton) — a GPU-process death no error handler can see. Every activation stage is now breadcrumbed synchronously; a leftover breadcrumb at the next launch means 3D killed the last session, so the editor says so, records which rendering strategy was in flight, and the next attempt takes the other one — the PIXI-shared WebGL context and a dedicated context each dodge a different class of Windows driver crash. If both strategies have crashed on a machine, 3D refuses to arm rather than crash again. The breadcrumbs then caught the actual culprit on the first native report: the editor injected three.js as inline script text, which parses synchronously on the main thread — and V8's recursive parser overflows the ~1MB Windows main-thread stack on a 2MB bundle (STATUS_ACCESS_VIOLATION) while Linux's 8MB stacks never noticed. Libraries now load through Blob URLs with external-script semantics, compiled off the main thread; a library-stage crash also no longer blames a context strategy, since no context existed yet.

- **Effekseer effects no longer draw black squares and slabs — every blend mode lands on the real scene.** Effects drew on a transparent overlay canvas, and each blend mode broke its own way: additive layers wrote alpha into empty pixels so black-backed textures composited as opaque black squares, multiply and darkening layers darkened nothing into solid black, and distortion panes had no background to sample. While effects play, the overlay now carries a copy of the whole rendered scene and the effects draw ON that copy — the same shared-framebuffer semantics the stock runtime has; the copy is pixel-identical to what it covers, so it is invisible in itself. Distortion panes capture their backdrop from that copy, aligned to each effect's own viewport. Verified by playing all 120 Demo animations and grading screenshots for black growth: the black-square class is gone, and what remains dark is darkness effects doing their job.

- **Editing an event no longer strips its model's texture.** Opening the event editor rebuilt the model spec for its pending pages and dropped the `texture` field, so pressing OK after any change — movement, a switch, anything — rewrote the sidecar without the colour map and the model stood gray in game.

- **A plugin that replaces `Tilemap.prototype.update` no longer blanks the tile layer.** MultiTweaks' "Tilemap animation speed" option installs a copy of the stock MZ update body; PIXI v5 tolerated that because it invoked `updateTransform` itself during render, but the PIXI 8 runtime runs repaint from its update tail, so the replacement left `_addAllSpots` unreachable — a black map with sprites, windows, and a clean console. `Tilemap.initialize` now installs a v8 `onRender` fallback that runs the shared `_prepareV8Frame` when the frame reaches render unprepared; a frame stamp keeps preparation single-shot so the Project3 double-preparation seams cannot return.
- **Encrypted RPG Maker projects display their images and audio in the editor.** Deployed MV/MZ games ship `.png_`/`.ogg_` (or `.rpgmvp`/`.rpgmvo`) files, so an imported game opened as an empty checkerboard, the tileset palette threw drawing a 0x0 canvas, and character previews stayed blank. The desktop asset resolver now decrypts those files on the fly with the key from `System.json` — recovering the key from the constant PNG header when the entry is missing — and asset listings present encrypted files under their plain names. Filename lookups also fall back to a case-insensitive match, since Windows-authored projects freely reference `bell3` for `Bell3` and a Linux filesystem does not. The runtime gained the same case correction: a Bitmap or WebAudio load that 404s retries once with the on-disk casing before reporting an error.
- **The fading splash screen no longer swallows the first clicks on the map.** It kept intercepting pointer events while transparent until its display switched off; it now lets clicks through as soon as the fade begins.
- **Opening an existing Reactor project updates the runtime that Playtest and deployment actually execute.** Editor releases update the application, not the `js/reactor_*.js` files already copied into every project. That allowed the 0.98.1 editor to open and playtest a project still carrying 0.98.0's shader bridge: its translator removed the `filterArea` declaration and rewrote `.xy`, but left `.zw` references undeclared, producing the unchanged `Could not initialize shader` and invalid-program flood despite the fix being present in the editor's canonical runtime. `reactor_main.js` now carries the engine version. Before game data is loaded, an older Reactor project receives the current canonical runtime recursively, preserves `reactor_plugins.js` and unrelated plugin files, updates its Reactor metadata, and is not rewritten again on later opens at the same version. Raw RPG Maker projects remain untouched until the explicit Install Reactor Runtime action.
- **Learnable Skills, Effects, and Enemy Action Patterns are directly editable.** The Class learnings table was display-only, while Skill/Item Effects and Enemy Actions put Add behind an undiscoverable context menu. Each now has visible Add/Edit/Delete controls, row selection, double-click editing, and Enter/Delete handling. Class learning edits author level, skill, and note; enemy Add uses a transaction-safe draft instead of silently inserting Attack, and HP/MP action conditions are presented as percentages while retaining the runtime's fractional data.
- **Extended face sheets are selectable throughout the editor.** Four columns and 144px cells remain RPG Maker-compatible, but the row count now comes from the loaded image rather than a hard-coded two. Actor list/detail previews, the Actor face picker, both Show Text face pickers, message preview, and Change Actor Images can preserve and choose indexes above 7.
- **The Sprite-based animation type can author a new MV animation.** Format detection now follows the runtime and keys off the `frames` array rather than requiring Animation 1 to already have a filename. Switching from Effekseer creates one empty frame, leaves both sheet pickers usable, persists the conversion, maps MZ `color`/`duration` flashes to valid MV combined timings and back, preserves imported `pattern: -1` cells, and refuses a seventeenth cell that the MV runtime cannot draw. New records remain MZ-format until Sprite-based is selected.
- **Troop Plugin Commands use `PluginCommandEditor`.** Insertion and editing of both MV code 356 and MZ code 357 now expose the same plugin, command, annotation, and argument controls available on map and Common Event pages instead of raw JSON.
- **Map event creation exposes the interactions that were already intended.** Event-mode double-click accepts the browser's click count and a 500ms fallback instead of requiring two presses inside 300ms. Enter creates on the selected empty cell or edits its event; context-menu rows show shortcuts; Ctrl/Cmd+F opens event search; and event copy, cut, paste, undo, and redo support Command on macOS. The Conditional Branch selector groups the complete stock MZ set separately from Reactor Advanced input conditions while preserving all existing command shapes.
- **Quick Event Creation builds the four stock MZ conveniences from an empty Event-mode cell.** Transfer opens Reactor's visual map/location picker and authors direction and fade. Door and Treasure use the project character picker, automatically suggest matching project assets and sound effects when available, and generate stock movement-route animation. Treasure can award gold, an item, a weapon, or armor before enabling an opened-chest self-switch page. Inn authors the offer choices, gold condition and deduction, fade/wait/recover sequence, and insufficient-funds branch at the chosen project currency price. The modal owns only a draft: Cancel or Escape performs no map mutation, commit revalidates the map and cell, and the complete event is one Undo step.
- **Simplified Chinese no longer lets generated guesses override reviewed terminology.** The deep catalog now fills only missing phrases. Reviewed forms restore terms including `职业`, `会心`, `注释`, `公共事件`, HP/MP commands, quantity, and Close; Turkish text accidentally copied into Chinese toolbar keys is removed; and `zh_CN`, `zh-CN`, `zh-SG`, `zh-TW`, `zh-HK`, and `zh-MO` aliases resolve to the matching script locale.
- **Database layout responds to the detail pane, not the outer window.** Container queries collapse generic section grids and form rows when navigation and the record list leave too little room. Translated labels wrap instead of ellipsizing, and the specialized Actor, Class, and Enemy compositions now reflow too; Class parameter curves use intrinsic columns rather than forcing four narrow graphs into half the workspace.
- **One-line plugin annotations no longer glue `@text` and `@desc` into the parameter name.** MZ3D writes `@param spacer|graphics @text<bidi>@desc ===` on a single line so RPG Maker can treat the bidi marks as a blank label. Reactor took everything after `@param` as the name, so the Plugin Manager showed `spacer|graphics @text @desc` and could not match the saved `spacer|graphics` key. The header, struct, and default parsers now split every known annotation on a line and strip format characters from values. Those spacer rows render as a rule, not as an empty field labelled with the internal `spacer|graphics` name. Section titles such as Map Settings that only exist to parent other parameters no longer show an empty box either.
- **MZ3D keeps imported character models and hides the 2D sheet from `pixi_compat`.** Cropping GLB materials to a walk frame smashed the authored UVs into a solid blob. `installMz3dCompat` only hides the 2D map sprites MZ3D already turned off via `_tilemap.visible`.

## [0.98.1] - 2026-08-11

### Fixed

- **The Web editor can load the map's 3D viewport dependencies.** The checkbox was not disabled; activation failed and immediately unticked it because `MapEditor3D.ensureLibraries()` only knew the desktop path: locate `runtime/` through `process.cwd()`, synchronously read `libs/three.js` and `reactor_3d.js`, and inject their source. WebHost has no Node `process`, and immutable project scripts are hosted assets rather than synchronous virtual-filesystem contents. The Web branch now resolves `project/js/libs/three.js` and `project/js/reactor_3d.js` through `host.assetUrl()`, loads them lazily and in order as classic scripts, shares an in-flight request across rapid toggles, and clears a failed request so it can be retried. Desktop retains its existing filesystem loader. Distribution coverage opens a fresh Web ZIP and verifies both files byte-for-byte against `runtime/` while confirming the outer editor page does not eagerly load three.js.

- **A saved 3D preference cannot make every later project open repeat a crash.** 0.98.0 wrote `map3DView: true` to the global `rr-settings` record before attempting library loading, geometry construction, WebGL2 context creation, shader compilation, or the first render. Deleting the original project therefore changed nothing: every project uses the same NW.js profile preference. Clean activation failure changed only the in-memory field, and a native Chromium/driver exit could not run rollback at all. A new process now migrates saved 3D state to false before the last project can auto-open. Each manual activation durably records false before graphics work and records true only after a complete build and initial render, so even a native exit leaves the next launch in 2D. 3D remains active across maps and projects in the current process, but every fresh process requires an explicit opt-in.

- **3D viewport activation and teardown are transactional.** Concurrent enable requests share one activation instead of creating multiple canvases and orphaning all but the last renderer. Disabling records the desired state and invalidates a pending activation even while scripts or images are loading. Renderer construction, the first rebuild, and the first render are contained; any exception resolves activation as false, restores the PIXI canvas, clears durable state, and reports the reason rather than becoming an unhandled rejection. WebGL2 is checked explicitly because the bundled three.js no longer supports WebGL1, and pixel ratio is capped at 2 to bound framebuffer allocation.

- **The 3D editor no longer creates a second WebGL2 context beside PIXI's.** Checking 3D on the bundled 50x50 Reactor One map could terminate the Windows NW.js process, including when the Windows build ran through Wine, while native Linux remained stable. That platform split follows Chromium's Windows ANGLE/D3D context path rather than map geometry. PIXI 8 explicitly supports sharing its context with Three.js through renderer-state resets, so 3D now pauses PIXI's ticker, renders Three into PIXI's existing canvas and WebGL2 context, and places a context-free overlay above it for orbiting, painting, and event input. WebGL1 is refused without probing or allocating another context.

- **3D renderers and stale map work are released deterministically.** Teardown disposes Three-owned scene and renderer resources without calling `forceContextLoss()` on the shared PIXI context, resets PIXI's cached GL state, restores its dimensions, redraws 2D, and resumes a ticker that was previously running. Project close and database-load failure tear down 3D before destroying the map renderer. Rebuild generations prevent an older map's delayed sheet, parallax, or character loads from committing after teardown or replacing a newer scene. A render exception stops the animation loop and fails back to 2D instead of scheduling another throwing frame at the display refresh rate.

- **The 3D editor preview refuses allocations that can terminate Chromium before JavaScript can catch an error.** The 2D editor supports 512x512 through chunked residency, while 3D still constructs the complete scene at once in ordinary arrays before copying it into GPU buffers. 0.98.1 limits preview construction to 40,000 cells and 400,000 estimated source quads, preserving the measured 200x200 production map while keeping larger or unusually dense maps in 2D with a clear status. Runtime map data and the game's own 3D behavior are unchanged.

- **MV-era PIXI filters no longer produce invalid PIXI 8 shaders during battle snapshots.** The v8 bridge replaced the legacy `filterArea` declaration and its `.xy` size reads with `uInputSize`, but left `.zw` origin reads untouched. Haven's Pixelate filter consequently compiled with an undeclared `filterArea`; the battle-background `Bitmap.snap()` path exposed the lazy compilation failure and then emitted `useProgram: program not valid` every frame. The translator now maps the logical origin to `uOutputFrame.xy`, maps legacy `filterClamp` to `uInputClamp`, handles precision-qualified declarations, and supports whole-vector or component reads. PIXI's six filter-global uniforms are excluded from the plugin `UniformGroup`, preventing zero-valued custom defaults from overwriting the real filter frame, input size, clamp, or output texture. The canonical runtime was synchronized into all local project templates; a real Project3 NW.js render of its bundled `PixelateFilter` completes with no shader messages and `glError: 0`.

- **PIXI 8 tilemap meshes no longer expose a blank, partial, or folded object seam while the camera pans.** `Spriteset_Map.update()` assigned the tilemap origin after `Tilemap.update()` had already synchronized its transform and mesh, so that update processed the previous camera position. The render transform could then detect the current position, repaint by clearing the visible geometry, and leave its newly generated commands dirty until the following frame. The current origin now reaches the tilemap before the child update cascade, and `_addAllSpots()` immediately synchronizes every lower, upper, or plugin-added tile layer before returning.

  One mixed phase remained: the PIXI 8 compatibility bridge also ran the complete plugin-wrapped `Tilemap.updateTransform()` chain from `onRender`, two or three times in a measured Project3 frame. `TF_Billboard` splits tall objects across 19 independently positioned and sorted row layers, so rerunning their positioning while PIXI constructed render groups could briefly fold adjoining rows even though every mesh was complete. Tilemap preparation now runs exactly once from `Tilemap.update()`; Window and TilingSprite retain the generic render hook they still require. A real Project3 mesh-backend smoke test completed a 360-frame diagonal out-and-back pan across 23 mesh layers with one preparation per frame, no hidden or dirty layer, and no fallback.

## [0.98.0] - 2026-08-10

### Added

- **An event can decline to join the object painted over its cell**, with `<3d ground>` in its note — or `<no 3d object>`, which says the same thing the other way round. Painting a group on the map takes everything standing on those cells and makes it one object, which is exactly right for the things that *are* the building: an animated sign, a lit window, a swinging shop door. They ride the building's plane and so hold still against it as the camera comes round, which is the whole reason grouping exists. It is wrong for anything merely passing through — a character walking behind a shop crosses its cells, and the moment they stop walking they are pinned to the shopfront and carried up it, because "is this part of the building?" and "is this standing on the building's square?" are the same question to everything except the author. A walking townsperson was already excluded by having left home; this is for the ones that stand still somewhere a building was painted. Honoured in the running game, in the pass that bakes events into the map, and in the editor's preview, so none of the three can disagree.

- **A parallax is chosen by looking at it.** Map Properties offered a dropdown of filenames, which is a poor description of a picture — parallax names run to things like `!Aeheri-Leader-Room`, enough to tell two apart only if you already know both. A **Browse…** button opens the editor's own image picker: the whole folder, searchable, with the current choice highlighted and scrolled to, and a full-size preview of whatever is selected. `(None)` is an entry in that list rather than a separate button, because clearing the parallax is the same kind of choice as picking one.

  The dropdown stays, since it is the fastest way back to a name you already know, and a thumbnail of the current parallax now sits under it with the image's pixel dimensions — which answers "is this the one?" without opening anything. Clicking the thumbnail opens the picker too. A name whose file has since gone hides the thumbnail rather than showing a broken image.

  The picker itself moved above the other dialogs. It sat at a z-index of 1001 and everything that had opened it until now happened to sit below that; the map properties dialog is at 10000, so the picker opened *behind* the dialog that asked for it — plainly triggered, with no way to tell.

### Changed

- **The language globe is a global control at the far right of the menu bar.** Its short code (`EN`, `JA`, `ES` and so on) shows which locale is active even when the translated menu labels are unfamiliar, updates immediately when the language changes in Options, and yields the code but not the globe on a narrow Web layout.

### Fixed

- **3D water moves in-game, mountain ranges keep their coverage, and mixed standing/flat objects meet cleanly without duplicate support meshes.** The geometry already carried animated A1 UVs and the editor advanced them, but the runtime render loop never passed its tilemap animation frame into the 3D scene. Runtime water and waterfalls now use the same clock as the 2D tilemap, while the editor uses elapsed time so 60, 120, and 144 Hz displays all advance at the intended rate.

  A repeating multi-cell foliage stand-in is one picture at its authored footprint rather than one complete object per constituent tile. Every source quadrant retains its physical map plane, and an incomplete stamp reconstructs the absent quadrants so a ragged fill edge cannot turn the lone mountain variant into half a mountain. A separate underlay at 0.6 opacity fills transparent silhouette gaps without contributing opaque depth. Declared structures preserve one composition frame and pivot across source layers and incomplete rows; missing cells can be reconstructed from that frame, standing rows and flat apron rows keep their roles, and the `edgeHinged` path prevents a declared facade from being shifted half a cell into its own footing.

  The lower and starred passes contain only their authored pieces: `_supportMeshes` and the runtime-only lower copy are gone. Fully opaque texels draw colour and write depth together in an exact-alpha core (`alphaTest = 1.0`); alpha from 1 through 254 stays in the depthless blended pass. This gives the depth buffer stable ownership without cutting soft edges or punching colourless holes into flat shadows. Source map layer remains the coplanar tie-breaker, and the editor renders lower geometry with events before clearing depth and drawing the starred pass, matching the runtime's lower/character/upper sequence.

- **The running game now batches its visible map into native PIXI 8 meshes.** The v8 compatibility path had restored tile rendering with one pooled Sprite per tile rectangle, which was correct but made every scroll repaint detach and reattach thousands of objects. Each physical `Tilemap.Layer` now emits its ordered commands into one typed geometry over a shared 4096×3072 nearest-neighbour atlas; shadows use a reserved atlas texel, animated autotiles rebuild with the same cadence as RPG Maker, and plugin-added tile layers receive both the tileset and the per-frame synchronization pass. Plugin children that are not tile layers survive clears and bitmap changes untouched, and Ultra Mode 7 still owns tile visibility while active.

  This is compatibility-safe rather than mesh-or-nothing. A layer automatically returns to the pooled Sprite path if atlas creation, GPU limits or geometry updates fail, and `window.$reactorTilemapBackend = "sprites"` forces that path for comparison (`"mesh"` requests the new path and `"auto"` is the default). `window.$reactorTilemapStats` reports builds, vertices, allocations, pool hits and fallback reasons. On a live WebGL run of Star Shift Rebellion map 596, a forced 180-frame scroll held mesh p99 at 5.7ms with 15.1ms total geometry-build work; the sprite path reached 22.2ms p99 and performed 222,234 pool operations. Both paths rendered with `glError: 0`.

- **Map dimensions are bounded before they can become an allocation.** The Map Properties fields advertised 999 as their maximum but never enforced even that: typing 10,000×10,000 proceeded to a 600-million-entry tile array, 5,000×5,000 could mutate resize state before failing to save, and 500×500 eventually asked PIXI to cache a 24,000px-wide layer. PIXI rounds cache dimensions to powers of two, so that became a 32,768² RGBA framebuffer, four GiB for one of as many as eight static layers, and WebGL discarded its contexts rather than throwing a useful JavaScript exception.

  Reactor now supports 1×1 through **512×512**, four times MZ's maximum map area. Larger maps use the viewport culler as an actual residency boundary instead of lazily materializing every offscreen tile a few seconds after load. The resident rectangle is aligned to 32-tile chunks with a one-chunk halo: ordinary movement consumes geometry already prepared around the screen, crossing its edge builds only the entering chunks and destroys leaving chunks as units, and unchanged meshes survive. Zoom and pan prepare destination coverage before changing the transform, so a frame cannot expose a missing chunk.

  Static tiles, autotile quadrants and animated A1 water are emitted as typed vertex, UV and index buffers grouped by source sheet and chunk, then drawn through PIXI 8's native WebGL mesh pipeline. A1 animation changes only mesh UV buffers every 30 frames, and painting rebuilds only affected chunks. This replaces hundreds of thousands of JavaScript display objects with a few hundred GPU meshes without allocating map-sized framebuffer caches. It also removes the visible-cell zoom restriction: minimum zoom is again the integer-pixel scale that fits the complete map. On Star Shift Rebellion's exact 200×200 map 596 data, which would expand to an estimated 312,141 tile sprites, true full-map zoom now builds in 147ms and leaves 461 display objects (219 meshes and 48 shadow sprites); twelve complete A1 updates average 2.05ms each and returning to a smaller resident window takes 5.6ms. A headless WebGL render with the project's real tileset PNGs completed at 0.0625 scale with all 40,000 cells resident, no context loss and no GL error; the previous all-sprite path did not complete the same full-map check within 15 seconds. Tile-layer caches independently reproduce PIXI's power-of-two allocation, query the active GPU limits, and decline anything over a conservative byte budget both when first cached and when painting expands a sparse cache.

  Typed sizes above 512, fractions and nonpositive values are rejected before new-map allocation, resize analysis or project mutation. Pasted maps and loaded files must have valid integer dimensions and exactly six complete data planes; imported files over 64 MiB are declined before JSON parsing. Full-map canvas previews and exports have their own pixel budget, and large-map undo depth scales down to keep whole-map snapshots within a bounded memory allowance.

- **Map JSON keeps its tile data inline, as RPG Maker does.** Ordinary saves expanded every tile ID onto its own indented line, paste compressed the entire map onto one line, and resize-reference updates used a third layout. The formats parse to the same object, so games were not functionally wrong, but the expanded tile plane roughly doubled real map files and added avoidable serialization, disk and parse work precisely where large maps already cost most. Every `Map###.json` writer now uses one hybrid layout: readable two-space properties and events, with only the top-level `data` array compact. The bundled Demo map falls from 145,984 to 70,981 bytes without changing one parsed value.

- **Event mode now outlines the cell under the pointer before it is clicked.** Empty ground and cells holding events use the editor's golden-yellow selection outline, so both creating and selecting have a visible target. Moving onto the cell already selected leaves its stronger persistent mark alone, leaving the map hides only the hover, and the first click on empty ground no longer clears its own selection while waiting for the double-click that opens a new event.

  The arrow keys move the persistent target one cell at a time and stop at the map edges. Crossing an occupied cell selects its event; crossing empty ground keeps the cell itself selected, so Ctrl+V puts a copied event exactly where the outline says it will and selects the new event afterwards. A map switch or Escape clears that target, and no target means no paste rather than an invisible fallback to `(0, 0)`.

- **Map shadows are composited where RPG Maker puts them.** The editor placed the shadow plane immediately after map layer 0, so an authored base tile on layer 1 could cover a shadow that RPG Maker draws above both base planes. Shadows now follow layers 0 and 1 and precede decoration layers 2 and 3, including in chunked large-map rendering.

- **PIXI 8 filter lists remain mutable through the legacy plugin API.** PIXI 8 freezes the array returned by `Container.filters`, so the common plugin sequence `this.filters = this.filters || []; this.filters.push(filter)` threw before an encounter transition could start. Reactor returns a cached mutable proxy and commits every push, splice, index assignment, deletion, or length change through PIXI's native setter, preserving PIXI 8's internal representation and its filter-effect attach/detach bookkeeping.

- **PIXI v4 geometry `copy` methods are restored with their original directions.** Point, ObservablePoint, and Rectangle copy their argument into themselves through `copyFrom`; Matrix retains the legacy opposite direction through `copyTo`. Existing native implementations are left untouched. This restores MV-era callers such as MVNovaLighting without silently reversing Matrix copies.

- **MV window padding follows plugin overrides again.** MZ's `updatePadding()` reads `$gameSystem.windowPadding()` directly, bypassing the `standardPadding()` method MV plugins replace. The MV semantics layer restores the original call so a zero-padding autosave window keeps its complete client area while ordinary windows still use the game system value.

- **MV font families declared in `fonts/gamefont.css` load under Reactor.** MV relied on a stylesheet to register names such as `GameFont`, while the modern runtime uses `FontManager` and previously registered no legacy family. Reactor reads valid local `@font-face` declarations and loads them through `FontManager`; browser-only play falls back to linking the stylesheet. When an imported MV project carries MZ's default font filenames but does not ship them, its declared game font is used instead.

- **A `ParticleContainer` draws the sprites put into it again.** v8 kept the name and changed what it renders: only `particleChildren`, which are `Particle` objects handed to `addParticle()`. Ordinary children are ignored outright, so a plugin written against the old API adds a hundred sprites, gets no error and no warning, and sees nothing on screen — the weather, the particles or the damage numbers simply never appear. It is now an ordinary container, which draws them. Nothing is given up: v8's batching needs `Particle` instances that no MZ-era plugin constructs, so those call sites could never have reached it. v8's own class remains available as `PIXI.__v8ParticleContainer`, and the MV spelling `PIXI.particles.ParticleContainer` points at the same place for MZ projects, where the MV compatibility layer is switched off.

- **A tile layer that a plugin added to the tilemap can draw again.** Through PIXI v5, v6 and v7 the tile textures belonged to the shared renderer plugin — one atlas, uploaded once, addressed by set number — so a layer drew from it whether or not it had ever been handed the tileset. That is what made it reasonable for plugins to add tile layers of their own, which billboard and overpass plugins have done for years. v8 has no shared tile renderer: each layer builds its tiles out of its own image list, and a layer that was never given one drops every tile it is asked to draw, with no texture to make and nothing to report.

  TF_Billboard moves every ☆ tile that *also* carries passage flags onto a layer of its own, so it can stand it up as a billboard. On a wooded map those are the trees, and each one lost exactly the tiles that plugin had taken — solid holes in the shape of the tiles themselves, a clean console, and an editor that looked perfectly correct, because the editor runs no plugins at all. The tileset now reaches every tile layer on the tilemap rather than only the two it built itself.

- **A tileset image that has not decoded is never made into a texture source.** The source is cached on the image element and lives as long as the session, so one built from an image measuring nothing stays 1×1 for good: every tile drawn from that sheet afterwards samples a single pixel and arrives as one flat colour, and the sheet finishing loading invalidates nothing. Painting runs every frame, including the frames before the tileset has arrived, so this was reachable in ordinary play — it turned a forest into plain green squares. Such a tile is now skipped and drawn on the repaint that follows the tileset actually being ready. Every window puts its contents inside a client area carrying an alpha filter, and hands that filter a rectangle so a scrolling list is clipped to the inside of the window instead of spilling over the border. Through PIXI v5, v6 and v7 the rectangle was screen-space and used exactly as given; v8 reads it as *local* to the container and applies the container's world transform itself. Handed the screen-space rectangle the engine has always documented, v8 transforms it a second time, and the region it captures lands about twice as far from the origin as the window — off the edge of the screen entirely for any window past the middle. The filter then resolves to nothing and everything inside the client area vanishes: the text, the cursor, the scroll arrows. The panel and its border are not in the client area, so they keep drawing, and the window renders as a correct, empty box with no error and nothing in the display tree measuring wrong.

  Reactor's own copy of that method had been changed to write the local rectangle v8 wants, which is why this was only ever seen under plugins — windows are a favourite thing to reimplement, and a plugin's version was written against the documented screen-space rectangle years before v8 existed. VisuStella's Core Engine has one, so a title screen built on its event title scene showed its frame and none of its six commands. Obfuscated releases cannot be edited in any case. The rectangle is now written in world space on every version, as it always was, and converted for v8 in a separate step afterwards — so whichever version ran last, ours or a plugin's, ends up clipped to the right place.
