- **No plugin's `WindowLayer.render` runs on PIXI v8, and none of them throws either.** That method masks each window with raw GL stencil calls, flushing a global batcher between them; v8 has no global batcher — each render pipe defers its own — so the calls never bracket the draws they were written for, and the stencil test is simply left switched on across everything drawn afterwards. Reactor's own copy returns early on v8 for that reason. A plugin that replaces the method does not know to, and VisuStella's Core Engine does, so it failed on the first missing call with a `forceStencil is not a function` on every frame. Shimming that call so it could run to completion was worse than the error: the whole masking dance then took effect and every window it was meant to reveal was rejected. The rule is now the class rather than the call — nothing deriving from `WindowLayer` has its replacement `render` bridged to v8 — and the missing framebuffer calls are stubs for the plugins that reach for them elsewhere.

- **A bitmap whose canvas has been freed is no longer uploaded to the GPU.** MZ releases a bitmap's canvas once it has an image to draw from, and a plugin calling `update()` afterwards asked v8 to send pixels it no longer holds: `texSubImage2D` received no canvas, WebGL raised `INVALID_VALUE`, and it repeated every frame for the life of the map. A source with no picture — no resource, or a detached canvas measuring zero — now declines the upload instead.

- **A plugin whose annotations were stripped out shows its saved parameters, in sections.** A plugin's parameter schema lives entirely in the `/*:` comment block at the top of its file, and obfuscated releases ship without it — of the forty-six VisuStella plugins in one protected distribution, not one still has an `@param`, an `@plugindesc`, or the comment block itself. There is nothing left to parse, here or in RPG Maker's own Plugin Manager, so the plugins carrying the most configuration are exactly the ones that appeared to have none.

  The values survive in `plugins.js`, and their shape says most of what the annotation would have: an object is a struct, a list of objects is a list of them, `true` is a checkbox, a number is a number, and a string that was stored JSON-encoded has to be written back that way. Read like that, one project's plugins produce 198 nested editors from 306 recovered struct definitions, so the settings open as the sections their author laid out rather than as one wall of JSON. VisuStella also writes each parameter's type into its own key — `QoL:struct`, `ActorHPColor:func` — for its own loader rather than for anyone reading the list; the suffix is dropped from the label.

  Reading a shape is a reading, and can be a misreading: an `:eval` parameter holding the source text `[255, 255, 0, 160]` looks exactly like a list and is an expression, and RPG Maker writes its own `__collapsed` bookkeeping into a struct as a bare array where every real field is a string. So each reading is checked by performing it — a parameter is offered as structured only when saving it reproduces the stored text byte for byte, and anything else falls back to a text box, which is what it would have had anyway. Across that project, 198 parameters round-trip exactly and 23 decline. What cannot be recovered at all is naming: which field is a colour, a switch, a file. Those stay plain text, and merely offer no picker.

- **A parameter declared `multiline_string` gets a text area.** RPG Maker 1.5's multi-line text type was rendered as a single-line box, which is a poor way to edit a paragraph. Unlike `note`, which is stored JSON-encoded, this one is an ordinary string and is written back exactly as typed.

- **Object designations can be painted again after switching project or map.** The 3D object overlay is built around the map's tilemap, and that is rebuilt per project — but the editor was handed the new one only when the Objects tab was *clicked*, and a tab that is already open is never clicked. Open a project with that tab selected, which is where the last session left it, and the editor was still holding the manager built for the previous project, or none at all. The paint path checks for one and returns quietly when it is missing, so a click produced no mark, no error and no clue it had been received. Every surface the editor paints through is now bound in one place, called both when the surfaces are rebuilt and when the editor arrives, so neither end has to remember the other.

  The Regions and Objects tabs also do their setting up when they are *selected* — building their panel, making their overlay layer, showing it — and nothing selects a tab on a project switch. An already-open tab is now re-opened, so the rebuilt surfaces arrive with a panel and a layer to draw on rather than neither.

- **An event can say which layer its animations play on**, with `<animation z: 6>` in its note, or `<animation over>` for the layer RPG Maker uses. Where an animation belongs is a question about the scene rather than about the engine — a spell cast over a character belongs in front of the furniture, a glow on a console set into a table belongs behind the table's own top — and one map will want both, so it is written on the event the animation is played on, which is the only thing that knows. The layers are the tilemap's own: 0 the ground, 3 the characters, 4 the tiles drawn over characters (the star-flagged pass, in 3D), 8 where an animation goes by default; a half above a layer sits clear of everything on it. Read in 2D as well as 3D, which had no way to place an animation below anything at all. Saying nothing keeps exactly the behaviour that was there before.

- **A tile samples inside its own square of the sheet at every zoom.** The half-texel inset added earlier fixes the boundary case — it moves the sample off the fence between two tiles — and cannot fix the far case: zoomed out, one screen pixel covers many texels and the sample can land in the next tile along. The inset would have to grow with the zoom, and the zoom is not a constant. So every quad now carries the rectangle it is entitled to and the fragment shader clamps to it, which is correct at any distance by construction. Mipmaps would have made this worse rather than better: a mip level of an unpadded sheet is built by averaging *across* tile boundaries, so the bleeding would be baked into the texture instead of merely sampled from it.

- **The 3D camera looks at the middle of the view.** It was given the exact centre point of the display, and the aiming code adds half a tile to whatever it receives — to turn the corner of a character's cell into the middle of it — so the camera looked half a cell past the middle and everything sat slightly left of where it belonged, by a margin just small enough to doubt.

- **An animation takes its place on the frame it appears.** The tilemap sorts its children in its own update and MZ creates animation sprites after the spriteset has updated its children, so a sprite added this frame stayed wherever it was appended — last, which means in front of everything — until the frame after. One frame is enough to see, and a looping animation restarts every few frames: three of them on a table flickered continuously between in front of it and behind it, and no amount of getting the layer right could settle it.

- **An event that is part of a 3D object sits on it, not half a tile above it.** The shader steps every cut-out half a cell towards the camera, because a thing standing on a cell fills it front to back and what the eye reads as "where it stands" is the near edge of that circle rather than its centre. Sprites were placed without that step, so an event pinned to a painted object was half a cell further from the camera than the art it belongs to — and under a pitched camera, further away reads as higher up the screen. A sign floated above its own board and took its animation with it. Only things standing on art take the step; anything on the ground is where it always was.

- **A parallax the 3D ground was built from is no longer also pasted flat over it.** A parallax plugin adds a layer per parallax to the tilemap, and the 3D ground sits in the tilemap too, sorted above them so a backdrop stays a backdrop. That is right for a starfield and wrong for the layer the ground was made from: the same picture was then on screen twice, once laid on the world and once pasted flat across it. Only the layers the ground actually took are put away — the map-pinned ones — so anything that scrolls or loops keeps drawing exactly as it did.

- **A new event can be made in the 3D view.** Right-clicking stopped at the event cube: a click that hit nothing returned, so the only cell you could open a menu on was one that already had an event on it — and *New Event…* lives on the menu for a cell that does not. Existing events could be edited and none could be created, which reads as the tool half working rather than as a case nobody had written. Bare ground now gets the same menu the 2D map gives it, on the cell the hover outline is pointing at, routed through the same calls so the cell is selected and the events panel follows. It still refuses outside event mode, and still stands down at the end of a right-drag pan.

- **One click of the shadow pen paints.** It did not: the pen paints a *quadrant* of a cell and worked out which one from the last position `pointermove` had recorded — a field the press handler never set. A press with no move before it therefore had nothing to read, and the pen took a missing position as "cannot tell which quarter" and declined. Dragging worked perfectly, because the drag's first move filled the field in, which makes this a strange thing to report and an easy one to dismiss as a slip of the hand. The press records where it landed now, which is the position that was always meant.

- **The editor's 3D preview draws the map's parallax.** The runtime asks ImageManager for it and there is no game running in the editor, so a parallax-mapped map previewed as its bare tile layers — which on a parallax map is very close to nothing at all. The editor loads them off disk instead. More than one is honoured: the map's own parallax and any `<MultiParallax>` block in its note, stacked in the author's `z` order, each map-pinned layer treated as ground and anything that scrolls or loops left alone as the backdrop it is. Layers added by a plugin *command* cannot appear — they do not exist until an event runs, so there is nothing for the editor to read.

- **A plugin that renders somewhere else and hands the picture to PIXI as a canvas now shows it.** This is the whole of MZ3D, which draws the world in babylon.js and passes the result through as a texture; under Reactor it drew nothing, and the map was the parallax sky it had been laid over. Two v5-era behaviours were quietly gone in PIXI 8, and neither leaves a mark.

  `Texture.update()` used to mean "push this to the GPU" — it called the resource's own update, which is where a canvas's *current* dimensions are read back off the element. In v8 it only recomputes UVs; the source has to be updated separately, and v8's own documentation says so. A plugin that builds its texture before sizing its canvas — which is the natural order, since the canvas is sized to the game and the game is not up yet — is therefore pinned to whatever the canvas measured at the time, and for a bare `document.createElement` that is the HTML default of 300×150. Reactor restores the v5 meaning: updating a texture updates its source, so the size catches up and the pixels arrive.

  The second half is that a Sprite used to track its texture, and in v8 it only subscribes when the texture is flagged `dynamic` — a v8 concept no MZ-era plugin knows to set. So the picture would have arrived correctly and been drawn into a 300×150 corner of the screen. A texture built from a canvas is now flagged, because a canvas handed to `Texture.from` is one the caller means to draw into. Textures from anything else keep v8's default, and Reactor's own `Bitmap` does not go through this path at all.

  Both are applied to the textures Reactor hands out rather than to every texture, which is not fastidiousness. PIXI's own Texture listens for its source's resize with `update()`, and a canvas source emits that *before* it has resized the element — so a blanket version reads a stale canvas mid-resize and puts the source straight back. The renderer's own view is a texture over a canvas, so the effect of getting that wrong is that `Graphics.resize` silently does nothing and the game draws into the bottom-left corner of its own canvas, with every measurement in the page still reading correctly.

- **A 3D map lit by PSYCHRONIC_RaveLighting is no longer hidden behind its own darkness.** A lighting plugin draws in two parts, and only one of them is the lights. Reactor was putting away `_lightContainer`, which holds the glow sprites, and leaving `_toneSprite` — a full-screen bitmap filled with the screen tone, with light-shaped holes punched through it. On a night or interior map that tone is `[-255,-255,-255]`, so the sprite is opaque black across the whole screen, over a 3D world that had already been lit for real.

  It also has to be told more than once. The plugin rewrites `_lightContainer.visible` from the options setting on *every* frame of its own `Spriteset_Map.update`, so a one-shot `visible = false` was undone before it was ever drawn. Reactor takes `renderable` instead and re-applies it each frame: `visible` is the plugin's, and writing it back would overrule a player who turned lighting effects off. Walking from one lit 3D map to another never crosses the on/off boundary either, so the second map's freshly built overlay used to arrive un-suppressed.

- **RaveLighting's lights are found where they are kept.** They were read out of the spriteset's `_lightContainer`, which holds additive glow sprites the plugin builds *from* the real configs — pooled, created lazily, left invisible when unused, and not built at all on a map whose overlay pass returns early. So a map with five lit braziers presented an empty container and Reactor put no lights in the scene at all. A light belongs to a *character*, on `_lights`, and that is now the only place it is looked for — the player and its followers included, each shape reading its reach from its own field, since a pulsating light records where it is rather than how far it gets.

- **The audio player's track list fills the dialog.** It was a flat 475px — the height of the A-Z strip standing beside it, 26 tabs at 17px plus padding — so the list was sized to fit the alphabet rather than the window, and everything below it was an empty grey band that could have been holding twice as many track names. The dialog is a column now and the list is the part that grows.

- **Verified candidate artifacts attach to the source release without replacing its notes.** The tag workflow normally creates the release body from the root changelog first. Artifact publication now detects that release, updates its title, and uploads all verified manifests and archives with `--clobber`; if source publication was interrupted, it can still recover by creating the release. The release workflow continues to verify all four manifests against the tag commit and sends the same bytes to GitHub and, when requested, itch.io.

- **The Web editor no longer begins with avoidable missing-file requests.** The editor page now declares its application icon, and the bundled MZ starter explicitly sets `$reactorMvCompat = false`, preventing the runtime from probing for an MV project marker or requesting MV-only `fonts/gamefont.css`. A served archive was verified through service-worker control, IndexedDB persistence across reload, and in-page Playtest reaching `Scene_Title`.

## [0.97.0] - 2026-08-03

### Added

- **3D objects are grouped on the map, not only in the tileset.** A tileset says what a *tile* is, and that is all it can say: an autotile id is a corner arrangement shared by forty-eight shapes, so three shops built from one wall kind are the same tile as each other and nothing in the tileset can tell them apart. Declaring a rectangle of a sheet does not help either — an autotile has no place in a drawing to declare. Which cells make up one building is a fact about a *placement*, so it is now said on the map, in a palette tab beside Regions and painted the same way. Cells sharing a number are one object however they are arranged, so a building hacked together from bits of three others is one thing and says so.

  It is per layer, because a tree on B standing over a wall on A is not part of the building; `Auto` paints every layer holding a tile, which is what takes a wall and the signage hung on it in one gesture. A **Footing** brush marks the cells that are the ground an object stands on rather than a course of its height — standing a drawing up turns its rows into courses, so a building painted across seven rows is seven tiles tall and plants itself on its southernmost row unless it is told which rows are the pavement.

  Every tool routes it: pencil, eraser, rectangle, circle and fill, with fill flooding by object number so a structure is grouped in one click. The eraser takes the grouping rather than the tiles under it. It lives in `Map###.r3d.json` beside the map, never in `Map###.json`, so the map stays ordinary RPG Maker data — which matters more here than most places, because MZ's six planes are full and there is no seventh to take.

- **A page set to Custom movement can say what the route is.** Choosing Custom was half of it: the type could be set and there was no way to author the route, so the page kept whatever list it already had and a new event kept an empty one — Custom meant "stand still" and could not be made to mean anything else. A **Route…** button sits beside the dropdown, enabled only for Custom, and opens the same dialog the Set Movement Route command uses, because an autonomous custom route *is* a move route built from the same forty-five commands. Two things the page has already answered are left out of it: the character, since it is always this event, and Wait for Completion, since nothing is waiting on it.

- **Flying the editor's 3D camera.** WASD moves, Q and E change height, Shift hurries. Movement is timed rather than counted, so the same press covers the same ground on any machine, and its speed is judged against whatever is in front of the camera when the flight starts. Orbiting is how you inspect a thing; this is how you inspect a place.

- **A hover outline in the 3D view.** On a flat canvas the cursor is already on the map and where a click lands needs no explaining; through a perspective camera the tile under the pointer is the answer to a raycast, which is not something you can eyeball. The cell is outlined as you move, scaled to whatever brush is in hand, so a three-by-three stamp outlines all nine cells rather than one.

- **Events can be dragged in the 3D view**, as they can on the 2D canvas, with the same refusals: not off the map, and not onto a cell another event already holds. Undo is captured on the first cell actually crossed rather than on the press, so clicking an event to select it does not push a snapshot of the whole event list and wipe the redo stack.

- **Events are drawn in a box in 3D.** An event drawn from a character sheet is a picture standing on the map and nothing about it says it is an event — it reads as a painted tree until you happen to click it, which is why the 2D canvas borders every event. Edges only: a filled box would have to be transparent, and transparent boxes are what sliced the name labels.

- **Escape lets go of whatever is held** — a lifted map stamp first, then a selected event, then the tile palette. Every selection was sticky and choosing something else was the only way out; carrying a stamp is easy to do by accident and it keeps painting on the next click. It stands down while a dialog or a text field is up, so closing a dialog does not silently drop your brush.

- **Clear, in the tileset's 3D Shape editor.** Every other setting there could be changed and none could be removed: Auto reset a tile's class and left its stand-in, its roof pairing and its object membership behind, with no way back short of hand-editing the sidecar. Clear forgets all four for every tile dragged over, works on autotiles — anything that can be classified has to be un-classifiable — and reports honestly, since the accessors return derived facts as well as authored ones and asking them naively marked every tile in the sheet as carrying information. It replaces Remove, which undeclared an object and kept the classes; the two overlapped almost entirely outside one narrow case and did not earn a second destructive button.

### Removed

- **The height brush.** Massing painted per cell was the wrong half of the problem: nothing on a real 3D map uses it, because the massing all comes from the tileset's 3D classes. Its toolbar button, level, action and width controls, undo integration and the 3D view's paint path are gone, along with eleven tests covering them. `MapElevation` itself stays — the runtime still reads elevation and the `<3d>` note from the sidecar — so a map that carries heights is unaffected; nothing writes them now.

### Fixed

- **3D grouping survived a save and not a reopen.** The sidecar was written correctly and nothing read it back: it was attached to the map only when the *3D view* opened, which held while everything in the file was something only that view could show. Grouping is painted on the 2D canvas, so the file has to be in hand whenever the map is. The visible half was an empty overlay; the silent half was worse, because painting again over an unread sidecar and saving writes one built from nothing and takes everything else in the file with it. It is now read as part of loading the map, and a corrupt one is logged and skipped rather than stopping the map from opening.

- **A grouped building counted as no change at all.** The dirty check compares what goes into `Map###.json`, which deliberately excludes the sidecar, so a map whose buildings had just been grouped read as untouched — closing the project never asked, and the work went without a prompt. The check now asks the sidecar too.

- **The Role tool changed nothing that lasted.** It cycled roles in memory and returned without saving, while every other tool in the 3D Shape editor saves — so laying an object's ground rows flat reached neither the file nor the open 3D map, and reopening the project stood them back up. It read as the tool doing nothing at all, which is a poor way to discover that roles are how a seven-row gateway is told which of its rows are the pavement.

- **Fifteen strings in the movement route dialog, and the whole 3D tool palette, were never translated.** The palette's tool names and hints — Select, Flat, Upright, Scenery, Foliage, Panel, Auto, Clear, Object, Role, Roof and every description beside them — were bare literals that went through no translation helper at all, so that palette shipped in English on every locale and the localization gate could not see it. They are routed now, translated into all seventeen non-English locales, and covered by the gate, which follows indirect helpers as well as direct calls.

- **The Region palette could not be scrolled, and neither could the new one.** A flex child defaults to `min-height: auto`, so it grows to fit its content instead of scrolling it: thirty-two rows of swatches in a panel a few hundred pixels tall with no way to reach the bottom. The tileset preview beside it has always said `min-height: 0` for exactly this reason.

- **Both palettes now sit in their panel the way the A-G tabs do.** The tab reveals the panel with `display: flex`, which is a row, so its one child was sized to its content and stopped short of the right edge — a bare strip beside the scrollbar that no amount of styling the canvas could close. They also draw at the project's tile size now, like every tile beside them, rather than at a fixed 48.

- **A region number was drawn at a fixed 18px into a canvas the size of a tile.** At 48 that fits; at 24 it fills the cell edge to edge, and at 16 it is not a number but a smear wider than the cell and taller. Four places drew one — both overlays and both cursor previews — and all four now size the glyph and its outline from the tile. The region overlay's texture cache was also keyed by number alone, so opening a 24-pixel project after a 48-pixel one handed it the previous project's textures.

- **The 3D view kept showing the previous project's 3D checkbox state**, and switching between the Region and 3D object tabs left both overlays drawn at once — two sets of coloured numbered squares over one map, answering different questions, impossible to tell apart.

- **Turning while flying threw the camera around an arc the width of the view.** `orbit` circles the point the camera is aimed at, which is right when you are studying a building and wrong when you are moving; turning now pivots in place. Turns are also banked and spent once a frame, because pointer moves arrive several to a frame and each one moved the camera, so a smooth drag came out as a burst of steps.

- **Orbiting pivoted about a point that had nothing to do with what was on screen.** The camera always looks straight at its target, so the pivot is at the centre of the view — but only as a *direction*. How far along it the pivot sits is the view distance, and nothing kept that honest: framing sets it to about the map's width, and flying then moves the camera without touching it. After a flight the pivot could be hundreds of tiles past everything visible, so a drag swung the whole map about a point out in the distance. A gesture now seats the pivot on whatever the centre of the screen is actually looking at, without moving the camera — the hit lies on its own view ray, so only the distance along that ray is corrected.

- **Cut-outs were built one cell wide however big their art was.** A stand-in names a rectangle of the sheet — a mountain drawn across a 2x2 block — and the span was read as an aspect ratio with its size thrown away, so that mountain was built at the same world width as a single tile. The picture came out squeezed to half its width, and the quad no longer covered the cell it stood on, so bare ground showed between one cut-out and the next: rectangular bites out of a range's silhouette, with the straight vertical sides a billboard quad has. A cut-out is now as wide as its art and as tall in proportion, it is never narrower than the cell it stands on, and its scatter is clamped to the overhang it can afford so a big one still breaks up the grid. On Infernis Prime, 7,515 of 8,550 foliage cells failed to cover their own cell and 3,821 adjacent pairs had bare ground between them, up to two thirds of a tile; both are now zero. A 1x1 stand-in multiplies by one, so ordinary foliage keeps its look apart from that size floor.

- **Star-flagged art wins where it stands in the same place as art that is not.** A column's capital carries the star flag and a plate resting on the same cell does not; both are cut-outs at one anchor, so they are exactly coplanar, and at equal depth whichever is drawn first wins and the other is rejected. Which of them that was came down to the order the meshes happened to be built in, so a capital could end up behind the plate leaning against it — the opposite of what the flag means, which in 2D puts the tile over every ordinary tile in the cell. The starred pass now carries a deeper depth bias, which decides ties between surfaces at the same depth and changes nothing about real occlusion: a column still goes behind a wall a tile in front of it. The runtime draws its two passes separately over a cleared canvas and never saw this; the editor draws the scene in one go, which is where it showed.

- **Standing art turns about its own axis, and stays seated where 2D draws it.** A cut-out's anchor is the point it pivots on, and it centred on the cell across the object's columns but sat on the *southern edge* of its bottom row along the other axis — so the two axes disagreed by half a tile. RPG Maker plants a sprite's feet on the bottom edge of its cell, `screenY` being `scrolledY * tileHeight + tileHeight`, and that had been read as a world position when it is a screen convention. Half a tile of pure depth is invisible head-on, which is why it looked right, and it swings into view as the camera comes round: a column standing in the middle of a three-by-three pool ended up planted on the pool's southern lip. Props, characters and the position plugins are told all take the middle of the cell now — they have to agree, or a character walking up to a column would not meet it.

  Centring the anchor alone then read half a square too far back from the front, because a column standing on a cell fills it front to back and what the eye takes for "where it stands" is the near edge of its base, not the middle. A cut-out has no depth to spread across, and which edge is near changes as the camera comes round, so no fixed world position satisfies both — the southern edge is seated head-on and adrift once turned, the centre is the reverse. The quad is offset half a cell towards the camera instead: the pivot stays the cell's middle, so the object turns about itself, while the visible base sits on the near edge from every angle.

- **Two standing objects can share a cell, as they do in 2D.** A cell has four tile planes and more than one of them can be standing art — a plate set down on a column occupies the same square as the column, and the 2D tilemap simply draws one over the other. The 3D builder asked for *the* upright tile of a cell, was handed the topmost, and threw the rest away: the plate was drawn and the column it stood on vanished, while 2D looked perfectly ordinary. Every standing tile in a cell is built now, lowest plane first so they layer the way the tilemap layers them. The questions that genuinely want one tile — which object a cell belongs to, whether two cells are one picture — still take the topmost, so nothing about grouping changes.

- **A cell can belong to more than one declared object.** A plate set down on a column shares both of the column's cells, and asking the cell's *topmost* tile which object it belongs to read the plate as the whole story — so the capital and the shaft joined the plate's 1x1 instances instead of each other. That made them two objects with two anchors, and a cut-out turns about its own anchor, so the column came apart and its halves swung independently as the camera moved. Each tile answers for itself now: the column's two cells find the same origin and are one object, the plates are their own, and the pieces of a column turn together.

  The star flag goes back to being read per tile, as the 2D tilemap reads it. Answering for the whole object was tried while a column was disappearing, on the theory that its halves were split between the passes; the cause was elsewhere — a cell only ever yielding one standing tile — and the object-wide answer had a cost of its own, putting a column's unflagged shaft in the pass over the characters where it covered a plate standing in front of it.

- **Painting a 3D class in the tileset editor takes effect, and is saved.** The Roof, Clear and Object tools each wrote the classification file when they were used; painting Flat, Upright, Scenery, Foliage or Panel did not. So the change lived only in the editor's in-memory store — not on disk, and not announced — which is why it appeared on the map only after the 3D view was switched off and on, that being what rebuilds from the store. Both the drag tool and the click-to-cycle path save now, once per gesture rather than once per cell.

- The 3D render loop lets go of its frame handle when it stops. `startLoop` refuses to start a second loop by checking that handle, so a tick that bailed while still holding a stale one meant the loop could never be started again — leaving a canvas that drew only when the pointer moved over it, those handlers rendering directly, and otherwise showed nothing.

- **Changes made in the Database reach the open map.** Passability, the star flag and the 3D classification were all edited in place and repainted only the Database's own canvas: the map editor holds the tileset object it captured when the map opened, so a flag change did not take effect until the project was closed and reopened, and a 3D class change did not until the map was next painted. Flag edits now announce themselves — `ProjectController`'s listener was already written expecting exactly that, its comment saying "on every flag edit as well as on an image assignment", and it was simply never sent — and the 3D classification gets an announcement of its own, `rr-tileset-3d-saved`, which the 3D viewport follows. Separate events because they cost different things: a stroke of the Shape tool would otherwise re-diff every sheet and refresh the database list per cell.

- **A project opened with the 3D box ticked comes up in 3D.** The view is a preference rather than a property of the map, and the viewport is torn down when a project closes — so opening another one left a ticked box over a 2D canvas, and only unticking and reticking put the two back in agreement. The controller now reconciles what is showing against what the preference asks for whenever a map comes up with the viewport off.

- **Which layer a tile lands on no longer depends on the tab it came from.** B started at z1, C at z2, D onwards at z3, and the search for a free slot then ran *upwards* from there — so the same object dropped from two tabs sat at two different depths, and a B tile went underneath a C tile for no reason but their names. RPG Maker's own maps settle it: across the bundled projects, of 256,366 cells carrying exactly one B–G tile, **90.8% have it on z3** and 8.9% on z2; of the 45,075 carrying two, **99.8% use z2 and z3 together**. B, C, D and E each sit in those proportions — the sheet makes no difference at all. Every picture sheet now starts at the top of the stack. Which *way* a cell fills is not something those counts can answer — two tiles end up at z2 and z3 whichever order they were laid down in — and it is settled instead by what the map draws: z3 covers z2, so painting one thing over another has to put the new one *above*, or the tile just placed vanishes behind the tile placed before it. So the top slot is always taken and the stack moves down beneath it, which is also what leaves that z2+z3 pairing, older underneath. The terrain slot is preserved however deep the pile gets, and putting something *below* what is already there is what choosing a layer by hand is for. The A tabs keep the ground slot.

  One exception, and RPG Maker has it too: an **overhang keeps its place**. The star flag means "draws above characters" — a canopy, an archway, the top of a doorway — and a plain tile painted into a cell holding one of those slides underneath rather than shoving it down, because the overhang is the thing meant to be in front. The authored maps bear it out: of the 14,066 stacked pairs where exactly one of the two is starred, the starred one is the upper of them 84% of the time. Two overhangs, or an overhang painted over a plain tile, follow the ordinary rule. Coverage in `picture-sheet-layers.test.cjs`.

- **A sheet assigned in the Database is usable straight away.** Assigning one to a slot updated the tileset editor's own thumbnails and nothing else: the map canvas and the tile palette each captured the tileset when the map opened, so neither heard about it until the Save button beside the name was pressed. A sheet added to E, F or G was therefore not selectable in its tab, and a tile placed from one drew nothing at all — the sheet it addresses had never been loaded, which reads as the tile vanishing. Assigning now announces itself the same way saving does. It only announces: writing `Tilesets.json` stays with the Database modal's own OK, so Cancel can still put everything back.

- **Walls cast their shadow, and take it away again.** RPG Maker fills the left half of the cell immediately east of a wall — quadrant bits 0x01 and 0x04 — and clears it when the wall goes. Reactor drew none: the only thing that wrote the shadow plane was the shadow pen, so a wall placed in the editor had no shadow at all, and one arriving inside a pasted stamp brought whatever had been lifted with it whether or not a wall was left to cast it. The rule is read off the authored maps rather than guessed: of 39,104 shadow cells across the bundled projects, 85.6% carry exactly that pattern and much the commonest thing beside a shadow is a wall immediately west.

  It runs on every path that lays a tile down or takes one away — the brush, the rectangle and circle area tools, bucket fill, the eraser and a pasted stamp — and keys off walls *coming and going* rather than off cells being touched — filling a floor beneath a wall that was already standing must not conjure a shadow the author never asked for. Only the two quadrants a wall casts are ever written, so a shadow put in the other half of the same cell with the pen survives a wall being built or removed beside it. A wall casts nothing onto another wall, roofs and ground autotiles cast nothing at all, and a wall on the map's eastern edge casts nothing off it. Coverage in `wall-auto-shadow.test.cjs`.

- **A pasted piece of map rebuilds its autotiles where it lands.** A stamp lifted with a right-drag carries the tile ids it was copied from, and an autotile id is a corner arrangement rather than a picture — so a stretch taken out of the middle of a wall arrived carrying middle-of-wall pieces, with no ends, and read as a wall with a slice cut out of it. The shapes are recalculated on paste now, as RPG Maker does, so the copy comes down as a finished wall of its own; the border is rebuilt too, so pasting against an existing wall joins it rather than leaving both with an end facing the other. **Shift** asks for the ids verbatim, which is what Shift already means when painting a single autotile from the palette — and it now claims the gesture while a stamp is held, where it was previously read as a map pan because the palette selection is cleared when a stamp is picked up. Only A1–A4 are rebuilt: A5 is not an autotile and B–G are pictures, whose ids mean a position on a sheet. Coverage in `map-stamp-autotiles.test.cjs`.

- **Auto-layer ground autotiles replace the ground they are painted over.** Sand painted across water was treated as an overlay merely because it was a different autotile kind: it went onto map layer 2, left the water underneath, and calculated its shape in a layer with none of the surrounding ground. It now stays on map layer 1, replaces the water, and reconnects every painted cell and neighbor there. Decorations still use the second A slot, and distinct A1 water kinds retain their deep-water stacking behavior.

  A tile also **joins the slot its own terrain already occupies beside it** before the fallback is considered. That keeps a repaired upper-layer road on the layer occupied by the rest of its run; moving it there now removes the stale lower copy instead of hiding a duplicate beneath the repair. A lower-layer decoration beside the cursor cannot pull a new decoration down through the floor at its destination.

- **The front of a mountain range stood a tile clear of the ground in 3D.** A connected region of upright cells shares one footing — its southernmost row — so that a wall does not tear in depth when its base is uneven. That footing was deciding *height* as well: a column's art was indexed from it, so a column whose lowest painted cell is north of the footing was drawn that many courses up with nothing beneath it. A range's southern edge steps back in ones and twos, so its whole front row hung. On Infernis Prime 166 of 616 columns hung; across that project's 906 maps, 10,207, some of them twenty-one tiles up.

  The two questions are separated now. The footing still sets the depth, which is what it exists for; height is asked separately, and a column only hangs where something is holding it up — an archway's panel has posts either side, so the rows beneath it are spanned by the same region left and right, while a range's edge has open ground on one side and sits down. Bridging counts only while it is unbroken, so a panel on posts standing on a slope is not read as floating. Across those 906 maps the run count and the number of courses drawn are both unchanged to the unit — no art is dropped or invented, it is only lowered — and hanging columns fall to 2,435, which are the genuine openings.

  The bridging test is reach along a row, so a connected spur of the same region further along reads as support. `reactor-3d-footing-lift.test.cjs` pins that limit as well as the fix: tracing what actually carries a load is the structure question the design notes record as unsolved, and this failure is one column of one shape rather than a whole range's front.

- **Panning past the edge of the map, then jittering back.** Holding the scroll wheel and dragging wrote the container position straight out and left the clamping to `updateScrollbars`, which is throttled to one call a frame — so the view was drawn genuinely outside the map and yanked back a frame later, every frame, for as long as the drag kept pulling. The view is held inside the map as it moves now, so a pan stops at the edge and stays there. The drag anchor is re-read from the clamped position each move as well: clamping alone banks the overshoot in the anchor, and pulling back then does nothing until it has been unwound, which reads as the pan sticking. `panBounds`/`clampContainerToMap` are the single answer to how far the view may travel — the same two `Math.max(min, Math.min(0, ...))` lines had been written out four times, for wheel zoom, the scrollbar thumbs and the scrollbar refresh, and not at all for the pan. Coverage in `map-pan-bounds.test.cjs`, which drives the real pointer handlers.

- **Shift could not extend a 3D object onto a second piece.** It only ever extended an object declared earlier in the same session, so selecting an existing object and shift-dragging the rest of it started a fresh object instead — which is the entire gesture, for a prop that crosses the seam where a B–G sheet's two halves are stacked. It now joins whatever is selected, read before the drag reselects, and a single shift-click onto an existing object is no longer refused.

- **An object anchored at a sheet's first tile could never be extended.** The merge asked `tileAtCell` for the origin and treated a falsy answer as failure — but the top-left tile of sheet B *is* id 0. The edge is checked by bounds now. The same call reported three different failures as one message, so a mismatch that was nothing to do with sheets said "that is on a different sheet"; each says which it is.

- **Dragging off the edge of a tileset claimed rows that were not on screen.** The drag never clamped, so it kept counting past the canvas and declared a rectangle over tiles the author could not see until they scrolled to them.

- The editor assumed 48-pixel tiles. `System.json` carries `tileSize` — MZ offers 48, 32, 24 and 16, and `DatabaseSystem2Editor` already exposed it — and `Game_Map.tileWidth` in the runtime reads it, but `TilemapManager`, `TilesetPaletteViewer` and `DatabaseTilesetEditor` all measured in literal 48s. A 32-pixel project's sheets are two thirds of the standard layout (512x384 where MZ would be 768x576), so every sample landed 1.5 tiles along and drifted, drawing a mosaic of unrelated art. `src/utils/TileMetrics.js` is the single source: `tileSizeOf` validates against MZ's four sizes and falls back to 48, `sheetScaleOf` converts a 48-based measurement. `TilemapManager.TILE_SIZE/WIDTH/HEIGHT` and the palette's `tileSize` are read from it and refreshed by `refreshTileMetrics()` once the database has loaded, since both surfaces are constructed before it. The 48s in tile-id arithmetic are untouched: there 48 means shapes per autotile kind, a property of the format rather than of the art, and a test asserts both that the pixel assumptions are gone and that the format constant survives. `TilesetPaletteViewer.prototype.tileSize` carries the default so an instance built with `Object.create` — which the transparency tests do, to avoid the DOM — still measures in whole tiles. `MapEditor`'s palette sampling, `ProjectController`'s map pixel extents, `MapEditor3D`'s character frames and raycast quadrant offsets, and `Reactor3D.MapScene.build` (via `Reactor3D.currentTileSize`, which reads `$dataSystem` the way `Game_Map.tileWidth` does) were converted in the same pass. `DatabaseSystem2Editor` calls `window.rpgReactorTileSizeChanged`, which `main.js` binds to re-read every surface and reload the open map, so the setting takes effect where it is made. Coverage in `tile-size.test.cjs`, including a sweep over all six renderers that fails on any bare pixel 48 while allowing stated `|| 48` fallbacks and the format's `% 48` / `2048 + kind * 48` arithmetic — the sweep is what caught the three sites in the tileset editor's autotile palette that a by-hand list had missed.

- The tileset editor's overlay marks were measured for a 48-pixel cell and did not fit a smaller one. The passage arrows sit a fixed 8 pixels in from each edge, so on a 16-pixel tile the left and right markers landed on the same point and all four directions read as one blob; a ladder icon 16 pixels tall starting 4 down ran into the tile beneath it; and the 3D class marks are sized as `tileSize - 26`, which at 16 and 24 is negative — and `fillRect` with a negative width draws *backwards*, into the neighbouring tile, rather than clipping away. `markScale()` brings the marks in with the cell below 32 and is capped at 1, so 48 and 32 are untouched: the marks are deliberately not shrunk down to 32, since one nobody can read is worse than one that is slightly large. Coverage in `tile-size-overlays.test.cjs`, which drives the real overlay through a recording canvas at all four sizes and holds 24 and 16 to the proportions 32 already has.

- Tile-size support is now checked by running the renderer rather than by reading it. `tile-size.test.cjs` sweeps the source for bare pixel 48s, which cannot tell a right formula from a wrong one built out of `TILE_WIDTH`. `tile-size-rendering.test.cjs` loads `TilemapManager` itself and renders every autotile kind and shape, every A1 animation frame, A5 and all six B–G sheets at 48, 32, 24 and 16, asserting that each rectangle is cut on the grid it belongs to, fills its cell exactly, stays inside its sheet — and, the load-bearing one, that the rectangle read at a smaller size is the rectangle read at 48 scaled by the same ratio the sheets themselves are. So a smaller project reads the same cells of the same layout, which is the whole claim.

- `EditorInstanceBroker`'s constructor threw in the web build, taking the editor down at boot with `Node module "os" is unavailable in RPG Reactor Web`. `WebHost` shims `fs`, `path` and `url` and throws for the rest, and the broker resolved `os` and `child_process` eagerly in its field initialisers — `start()` already returned false when any of them was missing, but construction never reached it. An `optionalModule` helper swallows the throw and yields null, so the broker declines on web exactly as it was written to. Coverage in `editor-instance-broker.test.cjs`, which loads the source against a require shim that behaves like the web host.

## [0.96.0] - 2026-07-27

Cycle overview: [RPG Reactor 0.96.0: A Deep Correctness Audit](../docs/devlogs/2026-07-25-rpg-reactor-0.96.0.md).

0.95.1 was an internal development version and was never published; its changes ship in 0.96.0.

### Added

- `runtime/reactor_3d.js`: the HD-2D renderer. A map opts in with `<3d>` in its note; `Map###.r3d.json` beside it carries elevation and camera, so no 2D project gains a file and a 3D map's `Map###.json` stays valid RPG Maker data describing its 2D footprint. The grid stays authoritative — `Game_Map`, `Game_Character`, passability, regions and the interpreter are untouched, and nothing in the 3D view feeds back into game logic. three.js (vendored to `runtime/libs/three.js` by `build-scripts/vendor-three.js`, absent from `scriptUrls`) loads on first entry to a 3D map, so a project with no 3D maps never fetches it. Compositing is by stacked canvases, the arrangement the runtime already ships for Effekseer: the 3D canvas takes z-index 0 under the PIXI canvas, so windows, pictures, weather and plugin sprites draw exactly as in 2D. `Reactor3D.Geometry` is pure — no THREE, no DOM — and produces one merged buffer per tileset sheet, which holds a 200x200 map to a handful of draw calls; autotile cells emit four quadrant quads picked from the same shape tables the 2D renderer uses. Upright tiles are collapsed into north-south runs so a building becomes one facade rather than a stack of per-tile panels. Coverage in `reactor-3d-foundation.test.cjs` and `reactor-3d-geometry.test.cjs`.

- Per-tile 3D classification, `Tilesets.r3d.json`. No heuristic over 2D flags separates buildings from terrain — on a real city map, flag-guessing produced facades fifty-one tiles tall — so it is authored, per tileset rather than per map since a tile is the same kind of thing wherever it is painted. `src/utils/Tileset3DClass.js` holds the store; `Reactor3D.CLASS_*` mirrors its values and a test pins the two together, as the editor cannot load the runtime module. `AUTO` is the *absence* of an entry, so clearing a tile deletes it and an emptied tileset drops out entirely: a fully cleared file equals a fresh one instead of accumulating husks. `keyFor`/`Reactor3D.classKey` fold autotile ids to their kind base, so one entry covers all 48 shapes — the difference between a few hundred lines and tens of thousands for a single tileset. Unclassified tiles fall through to the flag heuristic, capped by `AUTO_MAX_FACADE`, so an untouched map still shows something recognisable. The database loads and saves it either side of `Tilesets.json` and reports it through `getDirtyKeys`, since it sits outside `dataFiles` and would otherwise be lost on close without a prompt; the file is only written when non-empty. Authoring is a `tile3d` mode in the tileset editor that draws instead of the flag markers rather than alongside them, a tile already carrying up to seven glyphs. Coverage in `tileset-3d-class.test.cjs` and `tileset-3d-authoring.test.cjs`.

- The build preflights now require `reactor_3d.js`. It is in `scriptUrls`, so a project without it hangs at load waiting for a script that 404s — but `validateProjectRuntime` in `build.js`, `build-worker.js` and `dist-editor-worker.js` never listed it, so such a project passed validation and failed later. A test now derives the expected list from the manifest itself, so a future addition to `scriptUrls` cannot be missed the same way. `libs/three.js` stays out of both, deliberately: `Reactor3D.ensureLoaded` fetches it on demand and a project with no 3D maps should not pay for it.

- Painting in the 3D viewport, through `MapEditor` rather than beside it. `tileAt` raycasts the merged ground meshes and floors the world position into map coordinates — the map is one unit per tile, so a hit on a mesh shared by thousands of tiles still names one — and hands `MapEditor.beginEditState`/`paintTile`/`resetDrawingState` the same arguments the 2D canvas does, including a synthetic `lastMousePos` in tile-pixel space for the quadrant-sensitive tools. A left drag paints when `tilesetPaletteViewer.selectedTiles` is non-empty or a stamp/shadow pen is active, and orbits otherwise; Ctrl forces orbit. `resetDrawingState` commits the undo entry and fires `rr-map-edited`, which is what rebuilds the geometry, so a stroke needs no special path back. The cursor's tile is reported through `updateMapCoordinates` as in 2D.

- `applyAtmosphere` gives the scene a background and `THREE.Fog` scaled to the map's longest side (fading from 0.9x to 2.2x), taken from `--color-bg-base` — not `--color-bg-deep`, which is pure black on the dark themes and gives fog nothing to fade into. `MeshBasicMaterial` respects scene fog, so this needs no material change, and the runtime sets no fog of its own.

- Facade footprints get an apron. Every cell of a run is added to `consumed`, and those cells typically hold only the building's own art, so filtering out upright tiles left them with no ground quad: a fifteen-tile facade rendered a fifteen-cell hole to the sky. `nearestGround` walks south from the run — the direction it faces, so the first floor found is the street in front of it — then north, then along its row, and the result backfills every cell of the footprint. It takes `stack[0]`, the bottom of the layer stack: taking the top returned whatever decoration was drawn over the floor, which alpha-tests away to nothing and leaves the hole it was meant to close. On Moletown this drops the cells rendering as sky from 1,748 to 38, and those 38 are cells whose artwork is genuinely transparent — the 2D editor shows its own background there too.

- The 3D wheel handler calls `stopPropagation`. The 3D canvas is a child of `#canvas-container`, which carries `TilemapManager`'s wheel-zoom listener, so every scroll zoomed the 3D camera *and* the hidden 2D view, re-cropped its canvas, updated its scrollbars and overwrote the zoom readout with the 2D scale. `updateZoomReadout` now reports camera distance against `LABEL_REFERENCE` as a percentage while 3D is on, and teardown calls back into `updateMapZoom` to restore the 2D figure.

- Facade depth is a crossing plane, not a box. `uprightDepth` used to add two side faces and a cap; for alpha-tested cut-out art both are wrong — a side stretches one texel column across the depth, and the cap draws the tile horizontally over the full cell, which gave every spire and jagged roof a flat shelf the size of its footprint regardless of silhouette. The depth quad now runs at right angles through the cell centre, so both planes are ordinary billboards. Culled where either neighbouring column continues the run, so a wide building is still one flat wall. Two quads per level instead of four.

- Wall faces take the autotile's quadrants. `faceQuads` subdivides any face — cliff, rim or facade — by bilinear interpolation of its corners when the tile is an autotile, so the same four quadrants land on a vertical surface as on the ground. Walls previously used `sheetRectFor`, the block's whole top-left tile, which patched raised terrain with corner fragments of unrelated artwork.

- `Reactor3D.CLASS_SCENERY` (3) and `Geometry.build`'s `isScenery`. Upright collapses a run of cells into one facade as tall as the run, which is correct for a building drawn as a single perspective prop — Moletown's towers reach fifty-one tiles — and wrong for terrain, which is one tile repeated over an area. Classifying the Infernis world tileset's A2 terrain as upright produced 391 runs over eight tiles and a longest of 58; as scenery it produces 690 facades capped at 16 (the towns and towers, which really are pictures) and 7,979 single standing cells. Scenery raises its cell through `surfaceAt` rather than emitting geometry: a billboard per cell was tried first and turned a mountain range into rows of cardboard with daylight between them, because terrain covers an area and a picture does not. Raised ground takes its cliff faces from the existing wall code. `uprightPredicate` now returns false for *any* explicit class other than upright rather than falling through to the guess, which had let a scenery tile both stand on its cell and weld into a facade behind itself. Coverage in `reactor-3d-geometry.test.cjs`; the editor cycle is Auto -> Flat -> Upright -> Scenery -> Auto.

- The autotile shape tables ship inside `reactor_3d.js`. `Geometry.autotileTables` read them off the global `Tilemap` and returned `null` when there wasn't one — the situation the editor viewport and the offline preview renderer are always in, since both load the module alone. `autotileQuads` then returned null and `build` fell back to a whole-tile blit, stamping each autotile block's bordered top-left corner over every cell: on a world map, 63,620 shape-0 interior tiles that should have been seamless rendered as a grid of squares. A running game's `Tilemap` still wins, so a plugin that replaces the tables governs the 3D view too. The embedded copy is extracted from and pinned against `reactor_core.js` by test. The old test asserting the whole-tile fallback was pinning this bug as the contract; it now pins the four-quadrant path, and the fallback is pinned separately for a caller that supplies an incomplete table.

- `MapEditor3D.loadClassification` reads `Tilesets.r3d.json` off disk. The runtime fetches it by XHR relative to the running game, so in the editor `Reactor3D._classification` stayed null: every tile fell back to the flag guess, the facade cap then rejected anything over eight tiles, and `topTileAt`'s fallback laid the rejected wall art flat as ground texture. A classified city rendered as building facades painted on the floor.

- `Geometry.groundStackAt` replaces the single `topTileAt` call for ground, so all four planes of a cell are drawn bottom-up, each lifted `LAYER_LIFT` (0.001 tiles) above the last to keep the author's order without z-fighting. On Moletown 260 cells were dropping a layer. Wall skirts take the topmost ground tile's art. A facade's footprint now also draws the floor under the building — skipping it left a hole visible from above, since a facade is one plane at the run's southern end — and gets no wall skirt of its own, which would z-fight with the facade.

- `opts.uprightDepth` (default 0.5 tiles) extrudes facades into shallow boxes with a capped top. A single plane is paper: a chimney or a mast disappeared to a line when the camera came round. Side faces are culled where the adjoining column carries a run to the same level, so a wide building stays one flat wall and only its outer corners are boxed. The editor's default camera pitch dropped from 52° to 34°, since from overhead a standing facade is edge-on and the 3D view looked identical to the 2D one.

- 3D event picking: `MapEditor3D.eventAt` raycasts against the event cubes only — ground and facades are one merged mesh per sheet, so a hit there names a sheet rather than a tile. A pointer-up within 4px of its pointer-down counts as a click and selects; a drag orbits and selects nothing. Double-click calls through to `EventManager.editEvent`, the same entry point the 2D map uses. Coverage in `map-3d-viewport.test.cjs`.

- `Geometry.build`/`uprightRuns` take `isAuthored`, and `AUTO_MAX_FACADE` now judges guesses only. The cap was documented as never overriding an explicit `CLASS_UPRIGHT`, but `isUpright` reaches the builder as a plain boolean, so an authored tile was capped identically to a guessed one. On Moletown, 155 of 397 vertical runs exceeded the 8-tile cap and were dropped to ground — the tileset draws each building as one perspective prop up to 51 tiles tall — which is why a classified city still rendered flat. A run is now kept if any tile in it was authored, since a run is a single object and classifying its base classifies the thing. `MapScene.build` supplies the predicate from `Reactor3D.isClassified`. Coverage in `reactor-3d-geometry.test.cjs`.

- 3D events carry their sprite, name and the interactions the 2D map has. `eventSprite` crops the character frame into its own canvas rather than addressing it by UV, since `$` sheets are a single 3x4 block and normal sheets a 4x2 grid of them; sheets load once each per map (37 sheets for Moletown's 354 events). Sprites and labels billboard on yaw only — pitching them would lay a character flat when you looked from above. Labels rescale with camera distance so they hold their size on screen, and hide past `LABEL_DISTANCE` where they overlap into a band. Picking runs against `pickables`, which excludes labels: they are overlays, and selecting one fought with that rescale. Right-click routes through `onEventContextMenu` to `EventManager.showContextMenu` with the same arguments the 2D canvas passes. Selection is bidirectional via a new `rr-event-selected` announcement from `EventManager.selectEvent` — the single funnel the map, the events panel and the editor already share — so neither side needs to know who made the selection. Coverage in `map-3d-viewport.test.cjs`.

- `src/MapEditor3D.js`: the editor's 3D viewport, toggled by a checkbox beside the A1 toggle and stored as `map3DView`. three.js and `reactor_3d.js` are read off disk from `ProjectManager.getRuntimePath()` and injected as classic scripts at first use — the editor is packaged separately from `runtime/`, so a relative `<script src>` would resolve in the source tree and break in a distributed build, and a viewport with its own copy of the geometry would drift from the runtime the first time either changed. The 2D canvas is hidden, never destroyed: `TilemapManager` owns it and has sized and cropped it for the open map. Events become boxes coloured by trigger, the one thing a view of tile data cannot otherwise show. `MapScene.build` gained `options.flags`/`options.tilesetId` and `$gameMap` reads moved behind `typeof` guards, since in the editor it is not merely null but never declared. A completed stroke dispatches `rr-map-edited`, debounced to one rebuild, so the view follows edits instead of freezing on whatever was open. The toggle reflects what actually happened rather than what was asked: a missing runtime directory leaves the box unticked with the reason in the status bar. Coverage in `map-3d-viewport.test.cjs`.

- Tileset sheets F and G, in the tile-ID band `1024-1535` that RPG Maker leaves unallocated between E (ending at 1023) and A5 (starting at 1536). No MV- or MZ-authored project can contain an ID in that band, so the addition cannot collide with imported data, and `$dataMap.data` is unchanged — map files stay structurally identical. The band needs no new address arithmetic: `setNumber = 5 + Math.floor(tileId / 256)` already resolves it to slots 9 and 10, and that one expression backs both `Tilemap._addNormalTile` and `Sprite_Character.tilesetBitmap`, so tile rendering and event tile-graphics both work unmodified. The stock 8192-entry `flags` array already covers `1024-1535`, so passability, star, ladder, bush, counter, damage and terrain tags need no format change either. Sheets F and G behave exactly like B-E: 16x16 grids stored as two 8-wide halves, non-autotile, default to map layer 3, and participate in auto-layer stacking. Slot 11 is deliberately left unused — the pre-v8 renderer packs sheets four to a 2048px texture with `MAX_GL_TEXTURES = 3` and UltraMode7 reimplements that packing as `setNumber >> 2`, so slots 0-11 stay inside those assumptions while slot 12 would break them. Stored `tilesetNames` arrays are never migrated: an MZ-authored tileset keeps its nine entries and grows only when a slot beyond it is actually assigned, so a project that does not use F/G writes byte-identical `Tilesets.json`. Assignment goes through `RRTilesetSheets.setNameAt`, which fills any skipped slot with `''` rather than leaving a hole, because `JSON.stringify` emits holes as `null` and the runtime hands every entry of that array to `ImageManager.loadTileset`. The slot table, band bounds and sheet-base map live in the new `src/utils/TilesetSheets.js` and replace four duplicated `['A1'..'E']` literals; the map editor's four copy-pasted B/C/D/E tile-ID cases collapse into one case driven by that table. Coverage in `tileset-extended-sheets.test.cjs`, including a sweep asserting no authored map in the bundled Demo already uses the band.

- Collapsible event block structures: `Conditional Branch` (111/412), `Show Choices` (102/404), and `Loop` (112/413) render a fold arrow in the same slot and with the same affordance as the existing plugin-command expander. `findBlockEndIndex` matches the terminator on indent rather than by counting openers, so it agrees with the indent-aware structure parsing the rest of the editor uses, and a nested branch closing first cannot be mistaken for the outer one's end. Collapsed state is held in a `WeakSet` keyed on the opening command **object**, not its index: inserting or deleting commands earlier in the page shifts every later index, so an index key would leave the fold behind on an unrelated command. `collapsedHiddenIndices` scans outermost-first, so an inner block nested inside a folded one keeps its own state for when the outer reopens. A folded row carries a badge with the hidden line count so it is never mistaken for an empty branch, and a malformed structure with no terminator stays expanded rather than swallowing the remainder of the list. Coverage in `event-command-collapse.test.cjs`.
- Persisted event fold state: `EventCollapsePreferences` stores folds in `localStorage` under `rpg-reactor.eventCollapsedBlocks`, scoped by project path, map, event, and page index, so pages do not share state. Only deliberate folds are recorded and a fully expanded page deletes its record rather than storing an empty list, which keeps "everything open" the default for pages that have never been touched. Command objects are rebuilt from JSON on load and cannot be the persisted key, so entries are `index:code:indent`: pinning the code and indent alongside the position makes a stale entry self-correcting, since a page edited since the fold was saved simply fails to match and renders expanded instead of folding whatever unrelated command moved into that slot. Hydration runs once per page rather than per render — the live `WeakSet` remains authoritative within a session, tracking commands through edits, while the positional keys are rewritten from current indices on every toggle. Malformed or non-object storage degrades to fully expanded. `clearProject` drops a deleted project's pages. Coverage in `event-collapse-preferences.test.cjs`.

- Destructive map resize confirmation: `analyzeMapResize` reports the populated tiles falling outside the new bounds and the events that would be deleted, and `saveMapProperties` aborts without touching the map unless the listed loss is confirmed. Events outside the new bounds are now removed rather than left at coordinates the map no longer has, where they were invisible to the editor.
- Anchored map resize: a nine-cell picker in Map Properties chooses which corner or edge existing content keeps, so a map can grow from the top or left. `computeResizeOffset` turns the anchor into a tile offset and `resizeMapData` walks the destination, letting one expression serve every anchor; `top-left` yields a zero offset and is byte-identical to the previous top-left-only behavior, and remains the default on every open. Tiles and events move together, direct-designation Set Event Location commands on the map are shifted, and `System.json` player/boat/ship/airship starts on that map are updated. A non-zero offset also scans every other map file and Common Events for direct-designation Transfer Player (201) and Set Vehicle Location (202) commands targeting the resized map and offers to shift them, listing where they were found and naming any map that could not be read. Variable-designation commands resolve at runtime and are left alone. A live hint under the picker shows the offset and pending loss as the size fields change; `countTilesOutsideResize` short-circuits when the old rectangle lands wholly inside the new one, so the common grow case does not rescan six planes per keystroke.
### Removed

- The unreachable standalone tileset editor. `main.js` carried a `showTilesetEditor`/`closeTilesetEditor` pair that constructed `new TilesetEditor(...)` — a class that does not exist in `editor/src` and is loaded by no script tag, so the call would have thrown `ReferenceError` had anything invoked it. Nothing did: the two callbacks were passed into `DatabaseEditorUI`, which reads `updateStatus`, `getTilemapManager`, `showTypesEditor` and `showTermsEditor` from that bag and never the tileset pair. That dead entry point was the only route into `DatabaseTilesetEditor.initializeUI` and everything below it, so 19 methods — 1,220 lines, roughly 39% of the file — were reachable from nowhere. Among them was `drawFlagOverlays`, whose flag-offset table disagreed with the correct one in `getTileIndexForImage` on **every** sheet (A1 at 0 rather than 2048, A5 at 5888 rather than 1536, B at 6144 rather than 0) and which ignored the 48-slot stride autotile kinds occupy; had that UI ever been revived it would have drawn every passability marker from the wrong tile. A second pass removed `renderTilesetList` and `loadTilesetPreviews`, which survived on paper but queried `#tileset-list`, `.image-preview`, `.image-filename` and `.clear-image-btn` — elements only the deleted UI ever created, so both could only no-op — along with the `#tileset-name-input` fallback that the compact UI's own field had superseded. Reachability was recomputed from the three entry points `DatabaseEditorUI` actually calls (`constructor`, `cleanupListKeyHandler`, `showTilesetEditorDetail`, the last of which reaches `initializeCompactUI` through a nested instance of the same class): 35 methods remain and every one is reachable. Two tests were pinning the removed duplicates — a second tileset-only copy of the 250-row lazy-list batching that `DatabaseEditorUI` already provides generically for every database type, and the second of two terrain-tag readers — and now pin the single surviving implementation.

### Changed

- Bumped the current development version to RPG Reactor 0.96.0.
- Tileset layer list density and the empty-tab affordance. Eleven slots did not fit the left column: rows carried a 32px thumbnail with 6px padding and margin, and the list took a `flex: 1` half share of a column it split with the Flags panel, so it scrolled while the panel below had room to spare. Rows are now ~32px (22px thumbnail, 3px margin) and the list is `flex: 0 0 auto`, sizing to its contents and leaving the remainder to Flags. The "Normal (B-E)" heading became "Normal (B-G)", which the localization source audit correctly rejected as an untranslated phrase; the key and its letter range were updated across all seventeen non-English locales, with the Greek entry's transliterated `Β-Ε` corrected to the Latin `B-G` the tabs themselves are labelled with. An unassigned tab rendered nothing but a line of text, leaving double-clicking a row in the left column as the only way to assign a sheet; it now offers a **Choose Image** button in that dead space, using the existing translated phrase rather than introducing one. B-G are single-slot tabs and get one button; the A tab spans A1-A5 and gets one per sublayer rather than guessing which was meant.
- The tileset image picker uses the shared `rr-modal-*` chrome and the shared searchable browser. It was a hand-rolled overlay with an inline header, a red Close button matching nothing else in the editor, and a file list built one `div` at a time with its own hover handling and no search. It now composes `rr-modal-overlay`/`-header`/`-title`/`-close`/`-body`/`-footer` and delegates the list to `RRPickerIndex.createBrowser`, gaining live name search, Unicode section rails, sticky headers and keyboard selection for free. It opens on the layer's current sheet and scrolls it into view; Escape, the close button, Cancel and a backdrop click all dismiss it, and the document-level key handler is removed on close. Assignment funnels through a single `confirm()` reachable from a row double-click, a preview double-click, or the footer button, which starts disabled and cannot fire without a selection. A pinned **(None)** row sits above the browser rather than inside it, so clearing a slot cannot be filtered out of reach by the search box; because `''` is a legitimate selection meaning "clear this slot", the no-selection sentinel is `null` and the guard is an explicit null check rather than a falsiness test, which would have made (None) silently do nothing. The list column is a flex column holding that pinned row plus the browser, and the browser's own `height: 100%` is cleared before it is given a flex basis — the two rules cannot share a column, and leaving both in place stops the inner list scrolling and takes the section rail with it. The **+** button on each layer row is no longer gated on the slot being empty, since swapping an assigned sheet wants the same affordance. The (None) row's selected state uses `--color-accent-tint-15` and `--color-accent-border-strong` rather than `--color-selection-deep`, which is a fixed `#094771` that does not track the active palette and so read as generic blue against the gold accent text.
- Tileset passability markers are legible over any artwork. They are painted directly onto the tile art, so a pale glyph on pale ground — or the red X over red brickwork — vanished into it. `drawFlagGlyph` strokes each marker in near-black before filling it, with `lineJoin: round` so the corners of X and the star do not spike, and restores `strokeStyle`/`lineWidth`/`lineJoin` because the caller keeps drawing afterwards. All five markers (O, X, star, damage, terrain tag) route through it, and the O and X fills go from 0.6/0.8 alpha to 0.95 now that the outline supplies the separation rather than the wash. The remaining markers are shapes rather than text and get explicit edges through three primitives: `drawFlagDot` rims a filled circle, `drawFlagArrow` traces each chevron dark-and-wide before laying the colour over it, and `drawFlagRect` borders a filled rectangle. That covers the four blocked-direction dots, the four passable-direction arrows, the ladder stile, the bush berry and the counter bar; the ladder rungs and the bush centre stay unoutlined because they sit inside an already-edged shape and rimming them at 10x2px would be noise. A first attempt gave the shapes a soft `shadowBlur` instead, which proved too weak against busy or same-hued tile art — the hard edge is what makes them legible, and dropping the shadow also removes a per-tile canvas state change. `drawFlagArrow` restores `lineCap`/`lineJoin` for the same reason `drawFlagGlyph` restores its stroke state: both run inside a per-tile loop that keeps drawing afterwards. Coverage in `tileset-image-picker.test.cjs`, including a guard that `PickerIndex.js` and `AssetFiles.js` load before this file.
- Tileset edits reach the map and palette without a reload. `TilemapManager.currentTileset` is captured when a map opens, so a sheet assigned afterwards was absent from the array `loadTilesetImages` walks and its texture never loaded. `refreshTilesetImages` diffs the incoming record against the held one, reloads only the slots whose filename changed, drops only the `textureCache`/`a1AnimationCache` entries keyed to those slots, and repaints only when `mapUsesSheets` finds a tile currently on the map drawn from one of them — so assigning a sheet to a previously empty slot, the common case, costs one image load and no repaint. A first attempt called `renderMap()` unconditionally on every tileset save, which froze a 200x200 map; that is what this avoids. `ProjectController.refreshTilesetSurfaces` drives both surfaces and ignores saves against a tileset the open map does not use, and the `rr-tileset-saved` listener is installed once and debounced by 150ms because the tileset editor announces a save on every flag edit as well as on an image assignment. Coverage in `tileset-refresh-on-save.test.cjs`.
- `MapEditor.previewLayer` is a `PIXI.Container` with a `previewGraphics` child, rather than a `Graphics` doing both jobs. It is parented to on every pointer move, and PIXI v8 deprecated parenting to a Graphics, so each `addChild` ran a deprecation path on a node that does not batch like a container. Three sites also allocated a fresh `PIXI.Graphics` per tile — per pointer move, for every tile in a stamp or circle radius — purely to get draw ordering; `previewGraphics` is the layer's first child and already renders behind the sprites added after it, so they draw into it instead. `_resetPreviewLayer` replaces the old `clear()` plus `removeChildren()` pairs, which would now remove the graphics child and silently kill every rectangle drawn afterwards; it clears the vectors, and destroys the discarded sprites rather than detaching them, explicitly preserving their textures since those are shared with the tileset caches. Coverage in `map-preview-layer.test.cjs`.
- Map Properties identifies the map it is editing. The heading composes as `<base> | <id>: <name>` — `Map Properties | 0211: Canite City`, or the New Map heading with the id the map will take, which creation has already reserved. The name is read from the live `map-name-input` rather than the map record, so renaming in General Settings retitles the modal as you type while Cancel still discards the edit and the heading never claims an unsaved rename. It is written through `textContent`, so a project-authored map name containing markup cannot reach the editor's own interface as HTML. The heading is composed after `populateMapPropertiesForm` has filled the field, since composing earlier would read an empty name. The input is static in the document and outlives the modal, so its listener is rebound by stored reference — `removeEventListener` then `addEventListener` — rather than by the clone-and-replace the modal's buttons use: cloning restores the element's value attribute and would discard the name written into it moments earlier, and re-adding blind would stack a handler per open. A map with no id degrades to the name instead of printing a padded `000`. Coverage in `map-properties-title.test.cjs`.
- Map Properties tileset dropdown entries lead with the tileset id, `0211: Canite City`, so an entry can be matched to its numbered row in the database. Padding is four digits to match the Change Tileset command's picker, which already labelled tilesets that way; the two list the same database and would otherwise number the same record differently, and a test pins them together. The option `value` remains the bare id, so the prefix is presentation only and cannot reach the saved `tilesetId`. A tileset with no name now shows its id plus the shared "unnamed" label rather than the old `Tileset 7` fallback, which would have read as `0007: Tileset 7`.
- Map Properties sizing and layout density: the dialog was a fixed `height: 85vh` regardless of content, so short configurations reserved a band of empty space beneath them while expanding BGM, BGS, and Battleback overflowed and forced scrolling. It is now `width: min(96vw, 1400px)` with `max-height: 92vh` and an auto height, so it shrinks to its contents and scrolls only on genuine overflow. Map Name/Display Name and Tileset/Scroll Type each collapse into a two-column row. The hand-built left/right wrappers are gone: the panels are direct children of a `.map-props-columns` container using CSS multi-column (`columns: 340px auto`) with `break-inside: avoid`, so they pack and balance into two columns, dropping to one below roughly 700px. The count is capped at two deliberately: General Settings is a single indivisible tall panel, so a third column cannot balance against it and instead strands a short panel on its own beside a full-height gap. Grid was the wrong primitive here — it aligns rows, so the tall General Settings panel reserved matching dead space beside every shorter one.
